Abstract: Visual data are often expressed through multiple complementary modalities (e.g., images and segmentation masks), each capturing different aspects of the same underlying structure. Most existing multimodal methods adopt a top-down paradigm, learning a shared latent variable through joint training from scratch, which limits scalability. We propose Split and Bridge (SnB), a bottom-up framework for multimodal generation that couples pretrained unimodal diffusion models at sampling time. Rather than learning a unified latent space, SnB generates coherent multimodal samples by following a shared diffusion trajectory up to a split point and then branching into modality-specific trajectories guided by a bridge module. This design enables plug-and-play reuse of powerful pretrained models without joint training, making it scalable and easily adaptable. Across standard benchmarks (PolyMNIST, CelebAMask-HQ) and more complex settings (PIE-Bench++, ROCO), SnB achieves a strong balance between sample quality and coherence. We show that SnB naturally extends to downstream zero-shot tasks such as interleaved co-generation, image editing, and style coupling/decoupling. Our results demonstrate that bottom-up generation provides a practical framework that complements existing top-down multimodal approaches. Code is available at https://github.com/AhmadArrabi/SnB

Abstract: Video diffusion transformers generate high-quality videos, but attention over long spatiotemporal sequences makes inference computationally expensive. Sparse attention reduces this cost by computing only a subset of query-key interactions exactly, and recent training-free methods further approximate the remaining interactions. These methods, however, degrade quickly at high sparsity, which limits the achievable speedup. We present ESTRA (Extreme Sparsity Through Refined Attention), a training-free acceleration method that preserves generation quality even at extreme sparsity. ESTRA concentrates exact attention computation on a small set of important key-value tokens and approximates the remaining contributions with cluster representatives. To identify these tokens efficiently, it groups similar queries and uses their representatives to score individual keys. A variance-based correction further refines the attention weights of the cluster representatives, improving the accuracy of the approximation. Experiments on Wan2.1-14B and HunyuanVideo-13B show that ESTRA establishes a new quality-efficiency frontier for training-free sparse attention, achieving $3.26\times$ and $4.71\times$ end-to-end speedups at 5% attention density while surpassing Sol-Attn and SVG-EAR in fidelity to dense generation and on most VBench dimensions. Even at 1% density, ESTRA remains more faithful to dense generation than Sol-Attn at 12.5%, while reaching up to $5.39\times$ speedup.

</details>

Abstract: Microsoft Word is a central environment for office automation, and the faithful execution of its editing operations is an important capability for document agents. Unlike plain-text editing, editing in Word modifies a structured object model, where the requested effect is realized in object properties. Existing benchmarks, however, evaluate office workflows or desktop automation, treating word-processor documents as one task domain among many and verifying outcomes with keyword matching, per-task property checks, or screenshot comparison. We introduce WordEval, an operation-fidelity benchmark that instead centers evaluation on Word editing operations, testing whether each requested edit is realized as intended changes to Word objects and properties. It contains 642 cases across 15 Word feature areas, ranging from text and lists to charts, shape geometry, and SmartArt. 546 atomic cases cover 231 commands, 96 compositional cases combine multiple edits in a single document, and a Word-native oracle checks task-specific object properties and preservation conditions. Cases are constructed from editing-unit specifications, yielding controlled input documents, reference documents, and aligned oracle checks. Each compositional case is linked to corresponding atomic cases, supporting fine-grained diagnosis of editing failures. We evaluate eight agent systems and find that many struggle on the Atomic track, with performance varying across feature areas. Moreover, every system scores lower on Compositional than on Atomic, with gaps that vary substantially by system. Exact atomic-intent links and recorded failure diagnoses provide complementary views of this variation.

Abstract: The task of visual reasoning relative pose identification (VRRPI) has exposed critical weaknesses in the multi-view 3D spatial reasoning capabilities of modern vision-language models (VLMs). We present a systematic analysis of VLMs' behavior in VRRPI, uncovering stable output biases where VLMs disproportionately predict specific motion patterns regardless of visual evidence. To mitigate these inherent biases, we propose self knowledge re-expression with iterative debiasing (SKR-ID), an annotation-free pipeline that adapts VLMs for VRRPI using only randomly extracted unannotated image pairs. SKR-ID transitions VLMs' output mechanism from generic next-token prediction to task-optimized classification heads. The pipeline proceeds in two stages: (1) a cold-start initialization using debiasing object-centric prompts to anchor motion inference in 2D visual evidence; and (2) a recursive logit-ranking mechanism that enforces statistical parity by using the median logit value as a dynamic threshold. Experimental evaluation across frontier VLMs on standard benchmarks demonstrates that SKR-ID significantly mitigates systematic biases. Our method improves open-source VLMs' Macro-F1 scores from 35.0-45.4% to 53.7-58.0%, without using any annotated data. These results show that SKR-ID can effectively improve 3D reasoning performance from a severely collapsed baseline. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/astudentuser/Codes_of_SKR-ID.

Abstract: Image super-resolution in the dark is fundamentally challenged by extreme spatial heterogeneity: severely underexposed and noisy regions demand aggressive generative enhancement to reconstruct missing details, while relatively well-exposed areas require conservative updates to prevent hallucinated textures. Standard diffusion and flow-matching models, however, rely on globally uniform integration trajectories, making them sub-optimal for such spatially varying degradations. In this paper, we propose DeLang-SR (Degradation-Language steered Super-Resolution), a one-step flow-matching framework that explicitly decouples the restoration direction and magnitude. Rather than treating low-light corruption as arbitrary latent features, DeLang-SR translates measurable physical statistics into structured degradation language, harnessing the semantic prior of pretrained text-to-image models. The degradation-language intent, complemented by image-specific visual tokens, steers the velocity field (direction) toward restoration-relevant manifolds. Simultaneously, a continuous spatial update-strength field acts as a locally varying Euler step-size field (magnitude), applying stronger integration steps in heavily degraded shadows while enforcing conservative updates in reliable regions. Experiments on paired and real dark images, together with ablations and diagnostic analyses, show that DeLang-SR provides an effective and interpretable one-step route to super-resolution in the dark. Code and pretrained models are available at https://github.com/moriyaya/DeLang-SR.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-channel images encode heterogeneous channel semantics aligned over a shared spatial structure, which requires models to jointly capture globally shared structure and channel-specific variations. Recent multi-channel Vision Transformers attempt to address this challenge by augmenting the [CLS] token with memory tokens to organize multi-channel representations under varying channel compositions. However, we find that these context tokens collapse onto a few dominant channels under high inter-channel redundancy, producing redundant representations and a biased global summary that fails to reliably support composition-aware channel interpretation. We propose MuCa-ViT (Multi-Channel Composition-Aware Vision Transformer), a unified framework that addresses these limitations through two coupled mechanisms. Factorial Token Learning (FTL) enforces token-level decorrelation among context tokens, encouraging the [CLS] token to capture globally shared semantics while memory tokens preserve complementary channel-specific cues. Channel Composition-Aware Modulation (CAM) uses the FTL-refined [CLS] as a semantic anchor to generate sample-wise modulation signals, which enable composition-aware interpretation of channels within a unified backbone rather than relying on static channel embeddings. Experiments on JUMP-CP, CHAMMI, and So2Sat show that MuCa-ViT outperforms prior multi-channel ViTs across all three benchmarks, and the magnitude of improvement aligns with the inter-channel redundancy of each dataset ($ρ\in [0.19, 0.77]$), validating the diagnostic analysis that motivates our design. Our code is available at https://github.com/kimdanni/MuCa-ViT .

Abstract: Predicting where multiple agents will move next is fundamental to autonomous driving, robotics, and any system that must share space with people safely. Modern stochastic predictors read off inter-agent interactions once from the observed past and keep that view fixed throughout the generative rollout, so the reciprocal geometric constraints that emerge as the joint future sharpens are not re-examined. We address this gap with SRA (Spatial Reasoning Adapter), a modular graph denoiser that is integrated into existing diffusion and flow-matching predictors without redesigning their generative cores. At every reverse step, SRA reads the host's current future estimate, builds a sparse Euclidean interaction graph on the predicted trajectories, and returns a residual correction to the host's decoding path. Our adapter consists of three core components: a sparse neighborhood retrieved with a learned query-key score and a geometric bias, a future-conditioned relational feature recomputed on the evolving prediction, and uncertainty-weighted message passing where confident agents act as anchors for uncertain ones. Abstract: We present a hypernetwork that generates the parameters of an implicit neural representation (INR) for a given signal in a single forward pass, without test-time optimization. Existing hypernetworks for this problem either ignore the chain-rule structure of the test-time optimization that produces an INR or, when they preserve it, instantiate the structure with MLPs whose parameter count grows quartically in target-network width. Our central contribution is to show that this parameter-count cost is structural to the MLP instantiation, not to the optimization-oriented principle of preserving chain-rule structure, and to resolve it by replacing the MLP body with attention. The resulting hypernetwork combines three design choices: an output-neuron-centric tokenization that aligns the token axis with each layer's output-neuron axis; an information-flow consistency rule that fixes the role of every cross-attention module from the representation its output should occupy; and a token-space iterative rollout that realizes the five quantities one optimization step on the target INR computes as five cross-attention modules with parameter count independent of target-network width. Across five 2D and 3D INR generation benchmarks, our method outperforms the state-of-the-art single-pass and optimization-oriented baselines; scales to target-network widths at which the MLP-based optimization-oriented paradigm exhausts GPU memory; and produces the highest result on NeRF generation without ground-truth INR weights supervision.

Abstract: Stereo matchers estimate depth by comparing the left and right images from a stereo camera pair, but they assume that the two cameras are accurately calibrated and rectified. In real deployments, small physical shifts after calibration, caused by camera motion, mechanical drift, vibration, or installation changes, can break this assumption and degrade depth estimation. Online stereo rectification is therefore needed to correct the image pair during operation and recover a stereo geometry where standard stereo matching can work reliably. Recent online rectification methods mainly optimize row alignment, making the left and right views of the same scene point fall on the same image row. We show that this target, while necessary, is insufficient for deployable stereo depth estimation. A rectifier may make a small set of matched points look well row-aligned, but still distort the full images in ways that hurt stereo matching: useful image regions may be cut off, large blank areas may appear, objects may be stretched or squeezed, or image scale may change unevenly across the view. We propose VirtualRect, an online stereo rectification method designed for reliable real-world deployment. Rather than treating rectification as simply making the left and right image rows line up, this method estimates a rectified virtual stereo camera and combines image-wide supervision of image warp behavior. This makes the rectified images and the camera parameters come from the same geometry, so the images used for matching and the model used for depth computation remain consistent. Experiments on multiple datasets show that, compared with the prior state-of-the-art online rectifier, VirtualRect reduces vertical flow error by 17% on Carla-Flowguided and 7% on Semi-Truck Highway, and lowers depth MAE by 30-41% on Carla-Flowguided across three downstream stereo matchers.

</details>

Abstract: Latent-action world models learn actions from video without labels, and recent methods aim to share these actions across bodies. To address this, we introduce a transfer score that compares the correct action with wrong ones from the same source. In contrast, with a camera that follows the body, the best model reaches only 0.46 for an unseen animal family and 0.20 for humans. With this camera, a linear probe identifies the action class of unseen animal families with 66% to 86% balanced accuracy (chance 17%). The learned actions therefore retain information about which action is performed, but the correct action closes only part of the prediction error gap for a new character.

We propose PrivMark, a framework that suppresses biometric leakage at the image level. By analyzing VLM activations, we identify two weakly aligned steering vector directions in hidden-state space, each tied to a distinct leakage behavior --- one corresponding to incidental attribute disclosure under open-ended descriptions, the other to compliance with direct biometric probes instead of refusal --- and encode both directions into a single bounded pixel perturbation, so the protected image alone enforces privacy with no invasive access to model internals at deployment. To support evaluation, we assemble BioleakBench, $1{,}600$ VQA pairs covering biometric privacy attributes under both free-form description and direct-probe protocols. Across three white-box source models, PrivMark achieves up to $85.9\%$ biometric suppression while retaining $75\%$ of clean OK-VQA utility, up to $1.4\times$ the privacy-utility tradeoff of the strongest pixel-only baseline. The same one-shot perturbations transfer consistently to seven unseen VLMs including closed-source APIs.

Abstract: Visual Attention enhances convolutional neural networks (CNNs) by modulating intermediate features, yet their effects on downstream decisions remain less understood. We present a decision-space framework that models multiplicative attention as an intermediate feature perturbation and derives a first-order prediction of its effect on the target-versus-competitor classification margin. We further introduce the Decision Alignment Score (DAS) to quantify the directional alignment between the perturbation and the local margin gradient. We evaluate the framework on CIFAR-100 using SimAM and CBAM across five popular CNN backbones, ResNet-18, ResNet-20, ResNet-50, ResNet-56, and MobileNetV2. The analysis examines prediction agreement, DAS margin correlations, and downstream activation-state changes. Across most final-attention configurations, predicted and actual margin changes show good agreement, while DAS exhibits positive correlations with actual margin changes. Single-attention experiments further show that prediction agreement and DAS margin correlations depend on attention placement, with stronger associations at later stages.

The largest non-degenerate gaps reach 44.9 percentage points, with final bootstrap intervals confirming that the effect is not a point-estimate artifact. Abstract: Large language models can generate motion graphics from static images and natural language requests, but it remains difficult to evaluate how well models can follow requests that combine spatial, appearance, and temporal requirements. We introduce MotionGraphicsBench, an expert-curated benchmark of 100 instances for animating an input static SVG with a motion edit prompt. By supplying all required visual elements, our editing task reflects a designer-like workflow and isolates motion generation from graphic generation. Derived from real-world Lottie motion designs, our benchmark contains diverse combinations of spatio-temporal animation behaviors. Each benchmark instance pairs its model input with a deterministic, human-readable verification program that accepts different valid implementations and reports which prompt requirements pass or fail, rather than comparing against a reference animation or using a VLM judge. We construct these programs with a multi-agent pipeline using human-labeled animations and synthesized test cases. On a balanced test set of 180 animations, our programs achieve 93.3% raw agreement with human judgments, compared with 83.3% for a GPT-6 Astra VLM baseline, and outperform the baseline in both approval and rejection recall. Across 47 configurations from 32 LLMs and 12 providers, pass@1 ranges from 6.0% to 67.7%. Across 15 models evaluated at their highest available reasoning effort with 32 attempts per instance, additional attempts improve every model, but pass@32 still ranges from 38.0% to 95.0%. Our analysis of verification programs reveals that generated animations often fail to match reference poses, follow requested motion trajectories, or coordinate related events. We release our benchmark at https://huggingface.co/datasets/figma-ai/motion-graphics-bench.

Abstract: Long video understanding requires models to identify and reason over sparse, temporally distributed evidence within limited frame budgets. Existing keyframe selection methods typically retrieve query-relevant frames from a densely sampled candidate set before inference. However, global image-text matching incurs unnecessary computation when a small set of uniformly sampled frames already provides sufficient evidence. Query relevance alone may also overlook evidence that distinguishes competing answers, while one-shot selection offers no opportunity to correct earlier selection errors. We propose HypoKFS, a training-free, plug-and-play framework that couples keyframe selection with iterative inference by multimodal large language models (MLLMs). HypoKFS first reasons over sparsely sampled frames to construct an initial hypothesis space. The resulting answer distribution determines whether additional frame selection is needed. When needed, the retained hypotheses guide the selection of discriminative frames, followed by renewed inference and hypothesis refinement. This iterative process directs additional visual computation toward unresolved hypotheses while avoiding unnecessary global frame matching. Experiments on Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and MLVU demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple MLLM backbones. HypoKFS improves accuracy by up to 6.7 percentage points and achieves 72.9% accuracy on Video-MME at only 1.3% of the reference frame-hypothesis matching cost.

</details>

A Markovian context-pruned oracle-forcing protocol evaluates the dialogue turn by turn, injecting the gold answer before each subsequent turn so that per-turn reasoning is isolated from cascading dialogue errors. Abstract: Full projector compensation adjusts a projector's input image to compensate for both geometric and photometric distortions introduced by the projection surface. Existing methods generally treat the viewing condition as a static camera viewpoint. When applied to a moving viewer, existing methods face two key challenges: (1) view-dependent methods require model retraining or recalibration to compensate for novel viewpoints; and (2) NeRF/GS-based view-agnostic methods use structure from motion (SfM) to calibrate the projector-camera systems (ProCams) with an unknown global scale, while view-aware tracking requires calibrating a camera oriented opposite to the projector, which introduces an additional calibration challenge. To address these challenges, we introduce VAComp, the first view-aware framework for full projector compensation for a moving viewer in a naked-eye setting. Using a projector, VAComp enables the viewer to perceive a virtual object that visually replaces the real object or to observe view-consistent modifications of the real object's appearance and material properties. To resolve the SfM scale ambiguity, we use metric calibration as a reference to recover the global scale; to calibrate the oppositely facing camera, we use two checkerboards, one facing the RGB camera and the other facing the depth camera. To balance compensation quality and runtime efficiency, we develop two non-iterative hybrid methods, VAComp-SD and VAComp-SI. Both combine 2D Gaussian-based view-conditioned geometric correction with learned photometric compensation, predicting each compensation image through a single forward pass without per-frame iterative optimization. Real-world experiments show that both methods achieve competitive compensation quality, with VAComp-SD enabling real-time updates and VAComp-SI operating at interactive rates. Project page: https://realkaizhou.github.io/VAComp/.

</details>

Abstract: 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) provides high-quality real-time novel-view synthesis, but deployment budgets vary across devices and network conditions. Most compact methods target one operating point, so another budget requires a separate representation. SHIN is a scalable 3DGS hierarchy with a reusable Base and additive enhancement layers, so each higher-quality operating point extends rather than replaces the decoded representation. Constructing this hierarchy requires deciding which Gaussians each stage adds and how much spherical-harmonic (SH) appearance each retains, because these choices remain fixed in later prefixes. SHIN derives one shared Gaussian order from multi-scale position-scale sensitivity and divides each stage budget between adding Gaussians and retaining SH bands using calibrated gradient-based scores per byte. Lightweight opacity updates adjust compositing while transmitted geometry and SH coefficients remain unchanged. Across three benchmarks, SHIN progressively improves one cumulative representation and achieves the best PSNR-size efficiency in every overlapping comparison with the multi-rate and progressive baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Dense prediction and generative modeling in resource-constrained scenarios remain challenging, as repeated downsampling substantially reduces spatial detail and degrades boundaries. Although recent attention mechanisms have alleviated the problem of semantic ambiguity, they often fail to preserve high-frequency features or do so at the expense of a very high computational cost. In this paper, we propose the Frequency Guided Depthwise Separable Attention for Efficient Dense Prediction (FG-DSA), a novel framework for efficient dense visual understanding and generation. Abstract: Latent world models enable visual control by planning action sequences in compact representation spaces, but a gap remains between how latent dynamics are learned and how they are used for planning. First, teacher-forced training does not expose the dynamics model to the recursively predicted latent states encountered during planning. Second, sampling-based planners typically initialize action search from goal-agnostic proposals, without exploiting the action-conditioned dynamics learned by the world model. We introduce Dynamics-Informed Action Learning (DIAL) to bridge these two gaps. During training, rollout alignment exposes the forward model to its own autoregressive latent predictions, while multi-step inverse supervision trains a lightweight inverse dynamics head to infer actions from latent-state transitions. We further apply the inverse head to model-generated transitions with its parameters held fixed, encouraging predicted rollouts to retain action-relevant information. At test time, DIAL autoregressively couples the inverse and forward dynamics to construct a dynamics-informed action sequence toward the goal, which initializes the mean of the Cross-Entropy Method (CEM) proposal distribution. The subsequent CEM search then refines this initialization using the standard sampling and updating procedure. Experiments across four visual control benchmarks demonstrate that DIAL outperforms existing latent world models and planning baselines in both standard and long-horizon scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: Low-light conditions reduce visible contrast and obscure details, while infrared images lack color and texture, motivating the fusion of infrared and visible information for visual tasks. Existing fusion methods primarily optimize image appearance or task-specific features without explicitly disentangling the coupled factors underlying thermal observations, limiting their physical interpretation under thermal degeneracy. Hyperspectral thermal measurements enable physical decomposition, but they require specialized imaging hardware, while pixel-level physical annotations for supervision or evaluation incur additional costs. We propose TeX-Gen, a physically guided framework for TeX generation from paired visible and infrared images in low-light visual tasks. TeX Generation constructs complementary multi-view features and employs independent branch-specific gates to route cues into the Temperature, Emissivity, and Texture maps. TeXMetric integrates radiometric reconstruction, channel structure, and material consistency to assess the generated maps without requiring pixel-level physical labels. TeX Tune improves performance through task-guided adjustments, jointly optimizing the adjustment modules and task network while keeping the generator frozen. Experiments show that TeX-Gen achieves competitive task performance in low-light environments on the FLIR, LLVIP, M3FD, and MSRS datasets. Experiments on HADAR also examine the association between TeXMetric scores and downstream detection performance.

Abstract: Knowledge distillation (KD) between architectural families, e.g., from a Transformer teacher to a CNN student, is difficult because the two networks learn different representations. Logits share a label space, but heterogeneous teachers differ in logit offset, scale, and sharpness, and their non-target classes are less semantically organized. Conventional logit distillation matches these values sample by sample, asking the student to imitate how another architecture expresses its predictions. We argue that a heterogeneous teacher should pass on the relational structure of its predictions, i.e., how it arranges samples relative to one another, and propose Heterogeneous Asymmetric Contrastive Distillation (HACD) to transfer this structure. HACD maps teacher and student logits to centered, temperature-softened probability directions on a shared unit hypersphere, which removes architecture-dependent offsets and lets the temperature control how sharply peaked outputs enter the representation while keeping informative non-target classes. Each student output must then identify its own teacher target within the batch. Because every teacher target also acts as a negative for the others, HACD computes the targets on a moderately augmented view and contrasts both a moderate and a strong student view against them. HACD adds no learnable parameters and needs one teacher pass per sample. It achieves the best or second-best accuracy on 11 of 12 heterogeneous pairs on CIFAR-100, without ground-truth labels, and on 14 of 15 pairs on ImageNet-1K, improving over the reported vanilla KD results by 8.82 and 1.33 percentage points on average. Checkpoints are available at https://github.com/BTAEYANG/HACD-Heterogeneous-Asymmetric-Contrastive-Distillation; the code will be released upon publication.

</details>

Abstract: Recently, diffusion models have achieved remarkable performance in image generation. Abstract: Generating high-fidelity voxels while keeping their lightweight nature remains an open problem in computer vision. Current generators are either high-fidelity but computationally heavy, or lightweight but of lower fidelity. Hierarchical methods keep the lightweight nature of voxels. However, their coarse levels, which decide where all finer cells can appear, are generated with the least information about the shape and are never revisited, so they are often plausible but wrong. Our key insight is to let finer cells take part in deciding coarse cells. To this end, we propose JoVox, a discrete flow matching framework that learns the joint distribution of all levels of the voxel hierarchy, instead of a separate distribution for each level, so that coarse cells can be predicted with finer cells already generated nearby. Within this framework, we further introduce a two-level prediction, which outputs the cells two levels finer at once instead of one level finer, halving the number of levels that the network processes and yielding voxels of higher fidelity. We also introduce an unmasking strategy that revisits early decisions once finer cells are generated and corrects inconsistencies in masked diffusion models. Our framework outperforms the baselines on both unconditional and image-conditioned voxel generation. Compared with the current state-of-the-art TRELLIS.2, we achieve 8x higher training throughput, 1/15 of the FLOPs in a forward pass, train on 4 vs. 32 GPUs, and obtain higher quality with an 11% lower Chamfer distance.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models pass text-based false-belief probes at near-human levels but drop by over twenty percentage points on abstract panels the moment the same problem is conveyed visually. The consensus diagnosis is conceptual, prescribing new belief-reasoning modules. We argue the dominant bottleneck is perceptual: not what the model cannot reason about, but what it cannot see. We propose a five-condition dissociation protocol that holds the reasoning task constant while varying perceptual fidelity from visual only to text only, and quantify the result with the Perceptual Contribution Ratio, the share of the visual-to-text gap a given fidelity closes. We test this on two kinds of scenes: abstract 2D panels in the Heider--Simmel tradition, and naturalistic 3D game scenes from MuMA-ToM and MindPower. The evaluation covers seven VLMs and roughly 159,000 API calls. Ground-truth scaffolds substantially close the gap in every setting, and on the abstract panels and MuMA-ToM they match or exceed the text-only reasoning. Whereas scaffolds built by automatic parsers, including frontier VLMs, fail. The most common error in the visual-only condition is \emph{reality bias}, which accounts for 60\% of false-belief failures. Models report where an object actually is, not where an absent agent believes it is. This points to a perceptual front-end that fails to track which agents were present at each moment. Under the evaluated protocol, structured visual scene-state extraction, rather than belief inference, is the dominant bottleneck for visual ToM.

Abstract: While 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) is typically optimized from images only, many robotics and mapping applications provide both camera and high-definition LiDAR data, combining rich visual appearance with accurate geometric structure. We introduce PLS-GS, a probabilistic LiDAR scaffold for Gaussian Splatting that exploits this complementary sensing setting to construct a compact 3DGS representation aligned to the measured surface. PLS-GS fits a Hierarchical Gaussian Mixture Model jointly over position and color, yielding a continuous and adaptive scaffold that eliminates the need for discrete scene partitioning such as voxel grids or octrees. The fitted mixture then guides the renderable 3DGS Gaussians: each rendering Gaussian is associated with a GMM component and constrained in the local surface frame induced by that component's covariance. Across five large outdoor scenes, PLS-GS places primitives markedly closer to the LiDAR measurements than both image- and LiDAR-initialized baselines, while employing substantially fewer primitives, yielding a considerably more compact model and faster rendering. Abstract: Stroke-based rendering is often optimized as a pixel-level reconstruction task. This over-reliance on global error reduction not only degrades strokes into fragmented curves due to a lack of structured planning, but also allows locally defective primitives to be concealed by overlapping layers. To address these issues, we introduce a model-guided framework for stroke-audited rendering. Our framework uses Quadratic Bezier Splatting to analytically model continuous curved strokes with efficient differentiable alpha compositing. We further propose a single-stroke audit mechanism that explicitly evaluates local stroke quality, preventing defective primitives from surviving optimization through layer-wise compensation. Abstract: Image-text alignment models such as CLIP are typically trained with contrastive learning, where unpaired examples are treated as strict negatives. While this assumption introduces noise by penalising semantically related pairs, existing solutions often rely on heuristic similarity thresholds that lack statistical grounding and sensitivity to dataset-specific noise distributions. In this paper, we propose Permute-then-Adapt (PTA), a framework that leverages efficient weak models to identify missed positives while controlling Type-I error relative to a conditional permutation reference, up to calibration approximation error. Unlike standard distillation or arbitrary thresholding, PTA estimates a random-pairing reference distribution from the weak teacher's similarity scores and uses its upper tail, determined by $α$, to select auxiliary positives. The calibrated positive set is then trained against by a single multi-positive contrastive objective: each anchor maximises the total probability mass it assigns to its positive set. Offline calibration with the weak model adds negligible training overhead. Experiments show gains over heuristic soft-label approaches in object recognition, cross-modal retrieval, and data efficiency. Project repository: https://github.com/JinhaoLee/Permute-then-Adapt.

</details>

Abstract: With a solid theoretical foundation and pretraining on large-scale datasets, diffusion-based methods have achieved controllable generation leveraging text and image conditions, and generating excellent stylized images is possible using diffusion models. However, previous methods face significant challenges. First, because diffusion generation is iterative, the common approach of learning implicit style representations via cycle-consistency strategies is too costly and not well-suited to diffusion, even for LoRA-based methods. Second, decoupling style is difficult in the latent space of diffusion models. Representations of attributes are coupled in the latent space of diffusion models; thus, extracting style attributes with diffusion models usually suffers from the issue of content leakage. Furthermore, because style is hard to represent in natural language, capturing style using text prompts or textual inversion is deficient. In this paper, we propose a method for achieving disentangled stylized image generation through IP-cross-attention rearrangement, based on our experimental observations. We also propose negative content guidance, inspired by classifier-free guidance, to reduce content leakage in stylized image generation.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) are increasingly adopted as end-to-end driving planners, yet they suffer from cross-city domain shift. Existing methods treat this shift as a single quantity addressed by uniform alignment or single-site editing. We challenge this view on two counts: (i) domain shift in VLM trajectory planners is inherently structured across modality and layer, and (ii) a locate-then-repair recipe matched to this structure outperforms every uniform-objective baseline. We analyse domain shift via activation patching and uncover two regularities. (F1) Modality axis. Image and text tokens exhibit distinct shift patterns in each VLM. (F2) Layer axis. The layer-wise shift partition is architecture-dependent and dataset-stable. These findings recast domain shift as a two-dimensional tensor indexed by modality (image vs. text tokens) and layer, motivating a simple principle: locate where shift occurs, and repair only there. We instantiate this principle in MoLaRx (Modality-Layer Repair), a locate-then-repair framework. The Causal Importance Score (CIS) estimates the domain shift as a modality-layer tensor and partitions the layers into functional zones. CIS-Guided Selective Repair (CISR) then applies a matched operator (Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) / low-rank adaptation (LoRA) / freezing) to repair each zone, with the composition adapting automatically to each architecture. We evaluate MoLaRx on three architecturally distinct VLMs (LFM2-VL-1.6B, Qwen2-VL-2B, and PaliGemma2-3B) across two cross-city benchmarks (nuScenes and Argoverse 2). MoLaRx attains the lowest cross-city trajectory-error gap among adaptation methods in all six main-table settings, while using 50-60% fewer trainable parameters and 8.4-10.9 ms lower inference latency. In contrast, uniform MMD-only adaptation collapses in all six settings, highlighting the importance of structure-guided repair.

</details>

Abstract: Few-shot depth completion aims to recover dense metric depth from sparse measurements using only a small number of target-domain annotations, offering a favorable balance between annotation cost and model adaptation. We propose LIFT, a framework that harnesses both depth and feature priors from a frozen pretrained monocular depth estimation (MDE) foundation model for few-shot depth completion. Specifically, LIFT first estimates local scale and shift parameters on a coarse grid and interpolates them into smooth, dense parameter fields, providing a spatially adaptive metric depth initialization. It then progressively refines this initialization through multi-scale depth propagation guided by foundation features. Finally, a lightweight residual decoder combines multi-level foundation features with propagation correction to refine the metric depth. Extensive experiments on NYUv2 and KITTI-DC demonstrate that our proposed method achieves state-of-the-art few-shot performance. The source code will soon be released.

Abstract: Lane-level map updating requires temporally consistent, physically valid, and auditable road-scene semantics despite occlusions, ambiguous lane markings, temporary obstacles, and viewpoint changes. We present RoadState, a production-deployed vision-language system that transforms sequential road-scene observations into structured lane-level semantics for Baidu Maps' map-updating pipeline. Its core model, RoadState-VLM, formulates road-scene understanding as autoregressive state estimation, predicting the current structured road state from the previous observation and symbolic state together with the current observation. We introduce Geometric Chain-of-Thought (Geo-CoT), which uses a directed acyclic graph of semantic dependencies as a domain prior to improve semantic consistency and reasoning fidelity across interdependent road-state dimensions. Extensive experiments on a real-world dataset show that RoadState-VLM achieves 94.33% mean accuracy across six road-state dimensions, outperforming the strongest fine-tuned VLM baseline. Ablation studies further demonstrate the complementary effectiveness of Geo-CoT and temporal transition context. A cascaded routing pipeline directs routine cases to lightweight models while reserving complex cases for RoadState-VLM. Since its deployment in March 2025, RoadState has supported lane-level map updating across more than 360 cities and reduced overall production costs by 30%. Together with human-in-the-loop auditing, the system maintains robust performance under stress-test conditions. These results demonstrate how domain-prior-enhanced reasoning, structured state interfaces, selective routing, and human quality control can bridge the gap between VLM-based road-scene understanding and large-scale lane-level map updating.

Abstract: Medical foundation models (Med-FMs) are increasingly adapted to clinical tasks through prompt learning, which tunes a small set of prompt tokens while the backbone remains frozen. Recent work has shown that this efficiency introduces a vulnerability: jointly optimizing learnable prompts and an imperceptible noise trigger during few-shot adaptation implants a backdoor that leaves clean performance intact but steers triggered inputs to an attacker-chosen class. Since prompts are compact and readily shared, this raises two questions: whether a backdoored prompt remains effective on a different Med-FM, and whether it can be repaired without retraining the backbone. We address both in BAPLe++. First, we present the first study of cross-model transferability of prompt-based backdoors, quantified by two proposed metrics, Clean Accuracy Difference (CAD) and Backdoor Accuracy Difference (BAD). Second, we propose DePLe (Defense via Prompt Learning), which uses the infected prompt as a fixed anchor and learns a new prompt on a small clean set that preserves the clean task while diverging from the compromised embedding region. Experiments on four Med-FMs and six chest X-ray and histopathology datasets show that backdoors transfer across models, but asymmetrically and in a dataset-dependent manner. Compared with four training-free input transformations, DePLe reduces backdoor accuracy more consistently while preserving, and in several settings improving, clean accuracy. Our code can be accessed on https://github.com/Ufaqkhan/Baple-Plus-Plus

Abstract: Pretrained encoders from self-supervised representation learning are increasingly applied to sonar imagery of the seafloor, where they are evaluated with protocols that take one embedding per target. A target patch of seabed, by contrast, is re-imaged from an alternate look on overlapping survey passes, so each embedding is one measurement of that target. Standard protocols cannot say how consistent those measurements are, or how much of their spread belongs to the patch and how much to the acquisition geometry. We treat this as a measurement-reliability problem. Using a synthetic-aperture sonar (SAS) survey, we measure the repeat-look reliability of embeddings from a frozen natural-image encoder (DINOv3 ViT-S/16). Standard single-look metrics, effective rank, clustering and a linear probe for coral, are all stable to the look, and the probe performs well (AUC 0.93). Yet the repeat-look reliability of the same embeddings is poor, with a distance-based intraclass correlation (dbICC) of 0.20 and nearest neighbours and clusters that barely agree across looks. Variance components, calculated from generalizability theory, attribute under a tenth of the within-cell spread to look acquisition geometry. The rest is cell-specific and cannot be separated without repeat passes at matched geometry. A sonar-pretrained encoder (BenthicDINO) and a classical SAS texture descriptor show the same pattern. For this dataset and the tested representations, uses that depend on patch identity fail, such as cross-pass comparison and retrieval, while uses at the level of habitat type (e.g. coral classification) do not. Because the standard metrics did not detect this, we argue that repeat-look reliability should accompany any evaluation of representations of the seafloor.

Abstract: Visual generative models increasingly blur the line between synthesizing observations and simulating environments. Yet visual plausibility alone does not constitute successful world modeling: compelling outputs may lack persistent state, physical dynamics, or causally consistent responses to interactions. We define a generative world model as a predictive dynamical system that maintains a persistent, history-conditioned representation of an environment, updates that representation in response to actions or interventions, and generates temporally coherent trajectories that reflect outcomes under observed and counterfactual interventions. From this definition, we derive an intelligence-oriented framework spanning perceptual grounding, persistent state modeling, controllable transformation, dynamics-aware prediction, and closed-loop interaction. We present WorldFoundry, open-source infrastructure for reproducible inference and evaluation across video, 3D and 4D, embodied action, and interactive world systems. WorldFoundry unifies model and benchmark discovery, runtime and asset preparation, execution orchestration, artifact management, and interactive visualization, while modality-specific adapters preserve native inputs, execution semantics, output representations, and evaluation protocols. Explicit manifests, durable artifact contracts, and evidence-aware scorecards separate execution readiness from evaluative claims, making each claim traceable to its supporting artifacts. The catalog contains 270 model entries, 74 benchmarks, and 62 registered metric entries, with readiness and execution evidence recorded separately. WorldFoundry enables reproducible comparison of persistent, controllable dynamics across heterogeneous systems, advancing the field from perceptual synthesis toward measurable world intelligence.

Abstract: Camera rigs with identical measurement budgets can have different velocity estimation rates when exposure timing is uncertain. We establish this distinction for rolling-shutter reconstruction with two calibrated moving anchors, unknown scene points, and independently unknown auxiliary poses and clocks. On the same fixed local physical class near a separated lateral rig, repeated readouts have worst-case squared velocity risk at least of order $σρ_{\mathrm{eff}}$, whereas complementary readouts have minimax risk of order $σ^2$, for a fixed admissible clock displacement $ρ_{\mathrm{eff}}>0$ as the noise standard deviation $σ$ tends to zero. A finite physical compensation path gives the lower bound; a nonlinear velocity inverse proves complementary stability. All motion, structure, pose, and clock nuisances remain unknown. We identify mounting configurations in which both designs become regular and quantify the risk floor induced by metric uncertainty. We quantify full-parameter domains with independent 6-ms clocks and test in-domain reconstruction under paired noise and a depth-offset intervention. Raster studies examine image formation and calibration sensitivity.

Abstract: Video fusion aims to integrate complementary cues from heterogeneous sources, producing visually informative videos with coherent spatio-temporal structures. Jointly learning cross-modal complementarity and temporal dependencies typically relies on large-scale, high-quality video datasets, whose storage and repeated use during optimization incur substantial costs, making data-efficient learning highly desirable. To address this challenge, we rethink video fusion from the perspective of dataset condensation and propose STAR-VF, the first framework that condenses spatio-temporal representations for data-efficient video fusion. STAR-VF condenses the original training set into a compact set of learnable spatio-temporal representations, which are decoded into synthetic video clips and optimized using feature-level supervision provided by a frozen proxy model. The resulting condensed data preserves fusion-relevant spatial and temporal cues without requiring ground-truth fused videos as supervision. To adapt fusion learning to condensed representations, we equip the final fusion model with flow-free inter-frame temporal modeling and query-driven intra-frame aggregation, enabling direct temporal interaction and adaptive selection of fusion-relevant information in representation space. Extensive experiments on infrared-visible, multi-exposure, multi-focus, and medical video fusion benchmarks demonstrate that STAR-VF, trained with only approximately 1/16 of the original temporal training set, achieves fusion quality comparable to methods trained on the full data. Further analyses validate the contributions of learned condensation and flow-free temporal modeling, and demonstrate the robustness of the condensed supervision across different proxy encoders and downstream fusion models. Abstract: Image fingerprinting assigns each distributed copy a user-specific watermark for source tracing. Averaging collusion is a central threat: several recipients can average their differently marked copies to suppress each individual mark. Discrete bit-string watermarks are especially vulnerable because averaging drives bit evidence toward ambiguous decisions. We introduce \textbf{VectorMark}, which maps user keys to near-orthogonal high-dimensional unit vectors and identifies colluders by cosine similarity to the extracted watermark direction. Under ideal averaging of $K$ vector keys, the normalized average preserves a cosine signal of order $1/\sqrt{K}$ for each colluder, while unrelated keys remain concentrated near zero. The spherical geometry of random directions gives a shifted-Beta null distribution for cosine similarities, enabling analytic per-key false-positive-rate control after validation on unwatermarked images. VectorMark uses a neural watermark encoder and centered extractor trained with JND masking and a progressive curriculum for photometric, geometric, and collusion robustness. Experiments on MS-COCO, OpenImages, and AI-generated images show that VectorMark maintains robust watermark attribution under photometric and geometric distortions, and achieves the best strict all-colluder detection among evaluated methods, while preserving high visual quality. Our code is publicly available: https://github.com/nurlanov-zh/VectorMark

</details>

Abstract: Traditional text-based person retrieval (TPR) assumes that a witness provides a complete description at once, whereas interactive person retrieval (Inter-PR) refines a partial description through question-answer exchanges. In this setting, the questioner inspects retrieved candidates to decide what evidence to request next, while the answerer sees only the witness-side memory and cannot report candidate-specific visual differences. Existing methods mainly learn offline question orderings, leaving this visual-feedback loop underexplored. To address this limitation, we introduce visual-feedback interactive person retrieval (VFI-PR), a new interactive retrieval paradigm in which retrieved candidates produce visual feedback that becomes evidence for subsequent questioning and re-ranking. To support training and evaluation in this setting, we construct VFI-PEDES, a dataset and evaluation protocol for visual-feedback interaction, and propose VFI-ReID, a framework that learns to use visual feedback during interactive person retrieval. Through the Dynamic Accumulated-evidence Retriever (DAR) and retrieval-aware question optimization, VFI-ReID adapts retrieval and question generation to evolving evidence states, turning visual feedback into complementary evidence for subsequent retrieval. Experiments show that VFI-ReID consistently improves retrieval across VFI-PR interaction stages, demonstrating the utility of visual feedback for interactive person retrieval and outperforming existing Inter-PR baselines.

</details>

Abstract: Industrial anomaly detection is challenged by the scarcity and diversity of real defects, leaving insufficient anomalous data to effectively train downstream models. Existing anomaly generation methods often require product-specific fine-tuning, target mask annotation, or costly procedures to achieve realistic and diverse defects. We propose REDiff-AD, a framework that requires neither model training nor target masks and introduces Rectified Flow MMDiT into industrial anomaly generation for efficient sampling with fewer generation steps. Given a normal product image and a single masked reference anomaly, REDiff-AD employs in-context learning to transfer the defect appearance from the reference into the normal image. Internal attention signals generated during this process are further reused to localize the synthesized anomaly, while edge and color cues refine the localization into pixel-level pseudo-masks. Two lightweight modules further improve generation quality: adaptive reference conditioning enhances defect fidelity, while diversity guidance promotes varied defect patterns without additional training. Experiments demonstrate that REDiff-AD can well address the tradeoff between realism and diversity for anomaly pattern generation and achieve competitive downstream anomaly classification, detection, and localization performance. These results show that REDiff-AD is a simple, efficient, and effective solution for large-scale anomaly data augmentation and pixel-level data construction. Abstract: Forensic verification has converged on a binary "real vs. fake" label that confates fully synthetic, tampered, and AI-retouched images despite their very different consequences. We argue that GenAI manipulations decompose along two complementary channels: a camera channel of sensor fingerprints (destroyed by neural processing), and a semantic channel of scene-level coherence (disrupted by content edits). Each manipulation type leaves a distinctive two-channel signature. We instantiate this taxonomy in 2CAP (2-Channel Authenticity Protocol), pairing a contrastively-trained camera encoder with a frozen semantic encoder to serve two applications on a shared backbone: (i) four-class authenticity classification via cross-attention fusion with learnable reliability weights; and (ii) evidence generation, where an authentic image serves as a reference for a query and the two encoders produce per-channel alignment scores together with a patch-level saliency map. These signals are supplied as privileged context to a frozen Vision-Language Model (VLM) through an Observe-Generate-Refine loop, yielding explanations with localization without forensic fine-tuning. On a multi-source benchmark, 2CAP attains the best classification performance, including strong retouching detection where existing methods fail, and improves explanation quality.

Abstract: Surgical video understanding is an important foundation for intelligent assistance in robot-assisted surgery. Similar surgical views can reflect different actions or procedural stages. Interpreting these views requires access to earlier observations, yet frame-centric prompting leaves this history unavailable. We introduce MemSurg, a framework that remembers surgical observations before reasoning over them. Its structured memory graph stores timestamped entity categories, locations, motion cues, and interaction summaries. Chronological links preserve recent context, while entity-associated links connect recurring observations across the procedure. Task-adaptive retrieval then supplies evidence-grounded prompts for scene and workflow reasoning, supporting both frozen inference and memory-conditioned supervised fine-tuning. Evaluation on robot-assisted prostatectomy and two complementary surgical benchmarks shows a 10.43-percentage-point gain over SurgRAW with GPT-4o. Abstract: Data transformation pipelines shape the training distribution in computer vision, but designing them is a computationally expensive problem. We investigate whether large language models can enable efficient discovery by synthesizing executable transformation programs outside predefined augmentation spaces. We compare nine strategies under a unified training protocol, including standard augmentation policies, brute-force search, direct code generation with two LLMs, and performance-guided generation using LLMs fine-tuned on accuracy-ordered transformation pairs. When the best candidates are retrained for 20 epochs, LLM-generated transformation programs lead on every dataset, reaching 0.8877 accuracy on CIFAR-10, 0.5825 on CIFAR-100, and 0.9588 on SVHN, surpassing brute-force search over 6,000 candidates. The best transform on CIFAR-10 and SVHN comes from a fine-tuned generator using 270 candidates, while the best on CIFAR-100 comes from an off-the-shelf generator using only 10. This needs 22x fewer candidate evaluations in the generation step and 600x fewer for off-the-shelf generations, relative to brute-force search. Beyond transformation discovery, we analyze the reliability of the reduced-budget evaluation commonly used for augmentation search. Correlating one-epoch selection scores with 20-epoch outcomes reveals strong, weak, and absent predictive relationships depending on the dataset and architecture, with correlations ranging from 0.756 to near zero. A positive control indicates that range restriction alone is unlikely to explain the low correlations. Our results indicate that semantic synthesis with code LLMs provides an efficient alternative to search-based transformation discovery and highlight the importance of validating evaluation fidelity when optimizing data transformations.

</details>

By routing at the patch level rather than the token level, A$^{2}$MoE reduces routing complexity by orders of magnitude while satisfying load balancing by construction. Abstract: Small hand-tracking networks are deployed as 4- and 8-bit codes, and they should keep adapting to a user's camera after deployment, on the device and within roughly the memory of the model itself. Quantization-aware training cannot do this cheaply, because it keeps a 32-bit master copy of every weight together with its optimizer state, about 96 bits per weight. Training the codes directly with stochastic rounding needs no copy but injects rounding noise at every step, and the methods that remove this noise keep a residual buffer for every weight. We introduce sensitivity-gated accumulator caching (SGAC), which keeps residual state for only a small and changing set of weight groups. A set-associative cache of 8-bit accumulators collects the sub-step updates of the groups with the largest Fisher-weighted squared code step and writes them into the codes as whole steps; an evicted line is flushed by stochastic rounding, and a group without a line does not move until it is admitted. We prove that the write-back is unbiased and show, under a local quadratic model, that rounding a sub-step update costs loss of the same order as the update's own gain, so that rounding pays only where the expected gradient is large relative to the curvature. On a 383k-parameter hand-keypoint network, SGAC trains with 8.1 bits of state per weight. Scored on the 4/8-bit network that the device runs after adaptation, it has the highest mean accuracy of nine deployed low-bit networks on two simulated cameras. On twelve ImageNet-C corruptions it has the highest average of corrupted and clean accuracy of seven deployed methods, ahead of the PEQA-style, ECO-style and direct low-bit baselines with confidence intervals that exclude zero, and it matches an 8-bit accumulator kept for every weight with 56% of that state.

</details>

Abstract: Adaptive tokenization aims to reduce the compute spent on uninformative image regions while preserving accuracy. Abstract: Video understanding evaluations are intended to measure a model's proficiency in video perception and task solving. However, current VideoQA benchmarks struggle to isolate these capabilities due to their reliance on multiple-choice (MC) answer guidance, language priors, and fragile scoring pipelines. Together, our results show that a VideoQA score is jointly determined by language priors, option timing, and how outputs and labels are converted into correctness. We produce a novel Option-Delayed Replay (ODR) mechanism that discloses options at controlled stages of an open-ended (OE) trajectory, to obtain objective MC scores from faithful option-free inference. Our work demonstrates that agentic methods with ODR and faithful inference can match option-exploiting performance. Finally, we formalize our diagnostic collection and ODR as a reproducible evaluation protocol, and we release a human-relabelled edition of LVBench (correcting $20.5\%$ of keys) to remove annotation noise as a confounding variable.

Abstract: We present SoftPAT3D, a physics-augmented framework for text-to-3D scene generation in which deformable objects, such as cloth, stuffed toys and ropes, are generated as physically valid, simulation-ready bodies and placed in intersection-free contact with the furniture that supports them, with one another and with themselves. Existing physics-augmented pipelines simulate rigid bodies only and cannot accommodate a generated deformable object, which text-to-3D generation returns as a geometrically invalid mesh, sometimes self-intersecting, that has no rest shape, no material model and no admissible initial state. These are the inputs a deformable simulation requires, and we construct each from information already in the pipeline. The rest shape comes from how the object is manufactured rather than from the generated physically inconsistent deformed configuration, and a material library assigns each deformable object its initial elastic parameters, which can then be optimized. The occluded support is regenerated and converted into a watertight collision mesh, each object is released from an optimizable placement above the supporting part that the prompt specifies, and the complete scene is driven to static equilibrium under one unified contact model, with completion verified by geometric checks and a vision-language model. When a scene fails the prompt, a grid search over placements corrects it, and the adjoint of a differentiable simulation of the same contact energy reaches the targets the grid does not, places several bodies jointly and recovers and optimizes material parameters. Multi-body scenes equilibrate in a single simulation, and the pipeline also runs from the text prompt alone. On all sixty-five prompts, SoftPAT3D produces no undesired interpenetration of any kind, whereas every other system does, and its scenes directly support scene editing and robotic manipulation.

</details>

Abstract: Compressing continuous human motion into a finite vocabulary of discrete tokens is now the first stage of many motion understanding and generation pipelines. Vector quantization partitions a latent space into Voronoi cells around a finite codebook, but the geometry of that latent space is inherited from the input pose encoding, and it is unclear which encoding admits an efficient discretization. We study four encodings that place skeletal motion in different geometric spaces: Gaussian joint heatmaps on the pixel lattice, which we show to be a morphological dilation of the joint point set by a quadratic structuring function; root-relative joint positions in Euclidean space; local joint rotations on $\SO$ through a continuous 6D embedding; and a redundant full kinematic encoding. For each, we train VQ-VAE tokenizers whose codebooks lie on the unit hypersphere, varying codebook size, temporal stride, and code dimension. Beyond reconstruction, we measure codebook utilization and the effective vocabulary size, i.e., the number of equiprobable spherical Voronoi cells with the same entropy as the observed token distribution. Rotation and position encodings keep converting additional codewords into effective vocabulary, whereas the full kinematic encoding saturates at a few hundred effective cells and heatmaps at fewer than sixty. The smallest code dimension gives the highest utilization for three of the four encodings, and the effect of temporal stride depends on the encoding. Encodings of the same pose family can thus yield effective vocabularies differing by an order of magnitude, a difference that reconstruction loss alone does not reveal, which points to input geometry as a first-order design choice in motion tokenization.

</details>

Abstract: Visual in-context segmentation uses visual demonstrations to guide predictions for a new image. In SegGPT, we identify background color mapping deviation, where non-target regions sharing a background label receive different output colors, and part-to-parent expansion, where predictions extend beyond the requested part into its parent object. These deviations can occur even when the relevant regions remain distinguishable in internal features. Controlled changes to demonstration colors and requested targets support an account in which demonstrations guide role and color assignments over familiar semantic regions, influenced by learned semantic and color preferences. Building on these findings, we introduce Semantic Contrast Prompt Selection (SCPS). Its semantic contrast encoding assigns separate colors to the requested part and the rest of its object while keeping the remaining scene black. SCPS combines existing retrieval scores with quality estimates from labeled development images to select demonstrations and their binary or semantic contrast encodings. Averaged across seven retrieval methods on three benchmarks, SCPS improves target IoU by 0.054 and reduces leakage into the rest of the object by 22.7% relative to the original binary selectors. Our findings offer a new perspective on understanding and improving demonstration following in visual in-context segmentation.

These signed couplings allow alignment and opposition between latent spins to modulate local feature refinement. HistAttn attends over the current stage output and earlier representations at the same token position and adaptively blends the current stage output with a history-weighted feature aggregate without adding spatial pairwise attention. Abstract: Surgical procedures are inherently partially observable, requiring surgeons to continuously infer evolving anatomical dynamics and future surgical states beyond the current observation. However, existing self-supervised learning methods primarily learn from observed surgical videos through masked reconstruction or predicting representations within the camera's current field of view, lacking the capability to explicitly model the out-of-sight surgical dynamics. Inspired by latent predictive world models, we introduce SurgWorld, a video-native, self-supervised framework for surgical representation learning, which treats a specified spatial or temporal shift as a pseudo-action and learns to predict the target latent representation from a partial observation. Specifically, we propose Out-of-Sight Dynamics Modeling (OSDM) as the pretraining objective for predicting representations conditioned on relative spatio-temporal shifts. We further regularize the learned latent dynamics from OSDM with a Geometric Visual Similarity (GVS) objective by aligning patch-level relational structures between predicted and target representations, preserving spatial geometry and local visual coherence for dense perception. These two objectives are integrated through an agglomerative training framework that consolidates complementary supervisory signals from multiple teachers to learn a unified latent representation of surgical dynamics and visual geometry. Extensive transfer learning experiments on 17 surgical benchmarks show that SurgWorld consistently outperforms existing vision foundation models across surgical workflow recognition, fine-grained action understanding, and dense visual perception. We further conduct tailored qualitative and quantitative analyses, demonstrating that SurgWorld effectively captures surgical dynamics beyond the directly observed context through latent prediction.

Abstract: Parameter-efficient image-to-video transfer adapts frozen image foundation models to video via lightweight trainable adapters. Existing methods insert the same adapter at every transformer layer, implicitly assuming all layers require equal temporal modeling. A layer-wise sensitivity analysis across four video-native model families and two pretraining paradigms reveals that this assumption is wrong: temporal sensitivity increases with depth, and the pretraining paradigm determines its growth profile. Vision-only models exhibit a gradual rise, while vision-language models show early suppression followed by late recovery.
  This finding motivates STRAP, a training-free adapter placement rule that derives a spatial-to-temporal transition boundary from a paradigm-matched video model. Spatial adapters are assigned before this boundary and temporal adapters after it, without modifying the adapter architecture itself. On four fine-grained action recognition benchmarks, STRAP improves accuracy by up to 11.8% over uniform placement, reduces trainable parameters by 29 to 48%, and lowers inference time by up to 10%. On standard benchmarks, it matches accuracy with fewer parameters. Code is available at https://github.com/th-nesh/STRAP.

Abstract: Streaming 3D foundation models (3DFMs) estimate camera poses and dense geometry online, but they fall short of SLAM: their predictions are fixed once emitted, so loop closures cannot correct accumulated drift. With such models already providing strong geometric capability, a central question for SLAM becomes how to schedule their inference and how to keep its results as revisable state. We present Map2SLAM, which keeps LingBot-Map frozen, trains only a lightweight retrieval head, and builds SLAM on the model's inference interface, turning its predictions into observations of an external persistent SLAM state. The front-end tracks every incoming frame with the model's causal streaming inference and decides which frames are written to the persistent state as keyframes. The back-end asynchronously decides when and which past frames to re-observe, re-running the model's joint inference over them with fresh context; verified loop observations correct the trajectory online, and segment observations refine dense geometry after the sequence. All invocations share a single frame encoder pass per image, whose cached features also drive loop retrieval, so frequent re-invocation incurs no additional encoding cost. Map2SLAM achieves state-of-the-art performance on KITTI, VBR, and Oxford Spires, reducing mean ATE by over 40% compared with the best uncalibrated baselines, while maintaining real-time tracking at over 28 FPS at a resolution of $518\times154$ on an NVIDIA RTX 5090D GPU. Abstract: In the extreme-low bitrate range below $0.01$ bpp, image compression becomes a constrained generation problem: the reconstruction depends on inferring plausible missing details while staying close to the input image. Autoregressive token models provide an efficient coarse-to-fine prior for this setting, but the transmitted early-scale tokens often leave object identity and texture underdetermined, which can lead to source-inconsistent reconstructions. We introduce Source-Anchored Visual Autoregression (SAVAR), a generative codec built on Visual Autoregressive (VAR) modeling. SAVAR transmits early-scale tokens and a compact caption. The tokens provide image-specific layout information, while the caption supplies global semantics. SAVAR reconstructs a partial VQ-VAE latent from the retained scales, maps it to the start-of-sequence initialization, and uses the same latent to condition the caption embedding before adaptive layer normalization, separating two roles that are typically driven by a shared condition in VAR-style generation. On the Kodak and CLIC2020 datasets in the extreme-low range, SAVAR reduces perceptual distortion relative to matched ARPC retained-scale operating points, lowering LPIPS by about $8$--$9\%$ and DISTS by up to $22\%$ at a lower bpp. It reconstructs a $512 \times 512$ image in about $218$ ms on one NVIDIA L20X GPU, retaining fast autoregressive decoding in the evaluated setting.

Abstract: Visual chain-of-thought (visual-CoT), also known as "thinking with images", equips LVLMs with the ability to locate and use key visual evidence for reasoning. However, acquiring such visual-CoT capabilities requires costly post-training and tightly couples them to a specific backbone, making transfer to other LVLMs difficult. Existing cross-model transfer methods typically treat visual-CoT as a monolithic capability, using a unified representation that ignores the functional distinction between grounding and reasoning and introduces stage-irrelevant interventions. To address this problem, we propose Viscot-steer, a training-free activation steering framework for transferring visual-CoT capabilities across heterogeneous LVLMs. Specifically, Viscot-steer first disentangles visual-CoT into grounding and reasoning vectors and extracts them from the source-model layers associated with the corresponding capabilities. It then aligns the vectors with the activation space of the target model and steers the grounding and reasoning layers during bounding-box prediction and reasoning-trace generation, respectively. Across benchmarks covering high-resolution visual understanding, general visual perception and reasoning, and mathematical visual reasoning, Viscot-steer transfers capabilities from different visual-CoT models to architecturally diverse LVLMs and outperforms the strongest capability-transfer baseline by an average of 1.86 percentage points on the primary evaluation metrics.

Abstract: Accurate lighting response is essential for realistic dynamic human relighting, because shadows, highlights, and color changes should follow the target illumination in both direction and relative strength. Current learning-based relighting methods can generate visually plausible videos, but they often exhibit imprecise lighting responses: shadows and highlights may drift away from the target direction, and the output brightness may not faithfully follow the relative intensities in the target environment, making the result closer to appearance harmonization than controllable relighting. To address this issue, we propose DynOLAT, a generalizable OLAT video prediction framework for dynamic full-body humans. Instead of directly synthesizing a single relit appearance, our method predicts temporally consistent One-Light-At-a-Time (OLAT) basis videos from flat-lit inputs, and then linearly combines the predicted bases according to the target environment map. This OLAT-based formulation makes the directional response explicit in the predicted bases, while the relative lighting strength is determined by the linear weights derived from the environment map. We further introduce a dynamic capture pipeline and the first large-scale dynamic full-body OLAT dataset, comprising over 24 million frames from 224 identity-outfit combinations, where different outfits of the same person define separate combinations. Experiments show that DynOLAT responds more faithfully to variations in target lighting direction and relative intensity than the tested baselines, enabling controllable video relighting while preserving fine appearance details and temporal coherence. Abstract: Positron emission tomography (PET) is pivotal in medicine and healthcare, but requires high radiation doses for quality imaging. Although deep learning has shown promise for low-dose PET reconstruction/enhancement, existing methods primarily rely on fully supervised learning, which requires paired low- and full-dose scans that are clinically impractical to obtain. To overcome this fundamental limitation, we propose DuSS-PET, a novel self-supervised PET reconstruction framework that operates without any paired low- and full-dose data. Our method is built upon a computational Noisier2Noise (Nr2N) paradigm, featuring a Bernoulli corruption strategy that serves as an image-space approximation to emulate low-count noise patterns in image-derived pseudo-sinogram representations. The DuSS-PET framework integrates three core components: an adaptive Swin transformer for pseudo-sinogram restoration, a plug-and-play module for image enhancement, and a consistency-constrained SSL strategy that enforces agreement between the pseudo-sinogram and image representations. Extensive experiments on multi-center datasets demonstrate that DuSS-PET achieves superior reconstruction performance, outperforming state-of-the-art SSL methods and achieving comparable or even better results than fully supervised approaches across various dose levels. In particular, it exhibits exceptional generalization across different scanner vendors. This work is among the first self-supervised low-dose PET reconstruction framework that enforces consistency between reconstructed images and pseudo-sinograms, where the pseudo-sinogram generated in the image space serves as a solid computational consistency constraint for enhanced imaging without full-dose training scans. The source code will be publicly available at https://github.com/ladderlab-xjtu/DuSS-PET.

Abstract: Dichotomous image segmentation requires pixel-accurate foreground-background separation while preserving fine structures such as thin filaments, sharp edges, and intricate textures. BiRefNet addressed this challenge through bilateral references, combining image patches and gradient information, but its inward reference loses local spatial structure and lacks semantic guidance, while its outward reference relies on single-channel gradient attention. We recast this paradigm as controlled information recovery - locality determines what spatial information survives, semantic selectivity what information enters, and focused boundary supervision where refinement occurs - and propose BiRefNet-V2 to implement it: (1) locality-preserving and semantically guided detail injection through space-to-depth transformation, overlapping patches, and a Semantically Guided Gate; (2) a learned boundary stream with focused supervision that generates full-channel residual attention without requiring boundary annotations; and (3) a dual-branch decoder integrating depth-wise boundary convolutions with large-kernel attention. A modernized framework - LayerNorm, GELU, the Lion optimizer, and a DINOv3 backbone - cuts training from 600 to 50 epochs. Extensive experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on DIS5K, camouflaged object detection, and high-resolution salient object detection across all 13 evaluation sets.
