It first lifts frames generated between the endpoints with a transition-coupled 3D consensus that applies the static 3D prior jointly across frames, stabilizing unchanging regions and initializing the parts and joint. A rectified-flow kinematic refinement then lets the video prior supervise a 3D motion field over the whole transition, while part assignment and a shared joint constrain the field throughout optimization, keeping per-state errors out of the articulation. Finally, a motion-aware canonical appearance fusion uses the recovered articulation to map both endpoint images onto one canonical surface, texturing regions that only one state reveals. On PartNet-Mobility, TransArt3D outperforms methods given the same two endpoints in geometry and joint accuracy, and surpasses methods given six observed states in articulated-state geometry, joint localization and appearance.

Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) have demonstrated remarkable performance, yet their computational and memory demands pose significant challenges for deployment on mobile devices. Existing DFQ methods often synthesize calibration samples by maximizing the entropy of patch similarity. However, similarity entropy describes the dispersion of similarity values without ensuring that the generated samples cover diverse feature directions, potentially limiting their effectiveness for quantization calibration. To address this limitation, we propose RankQ-ViT, a rank-guided DFQ framework for ViTs. We first observe that patch similarity matrices derived from Gaussian noise tend to have lower ranks than those derived from real images. Abstract: Salient and camouflaged object detection (SOD and COD) both recover foreground support, yet most joint methods output only a binary mask and therefore cannot distinguish the type. USC12K distinguishes the two types, but its baseline USCNet still binds foreground existence to the type within a single normalization, so the failure source cannot be attributed. Our cross-task study shows that foreground localization transfers between SOD and COD, whereas type discrimination does not: a COD-only model recovers foreground on SOD images but its type judgement follows the training distribution rather than the foreground-background relation, so the two decisions require separate mechanisms. SegDis factorizes the output into an explicit foreground probability and a conditional type probability given foreground: FLR provides high-quality task-agnostic support, and RTA refines the SOD/COD assignment from the foreground-background relation without a task identifier. Because support is explicit, each ground-truth foreground pixel is classified as correct-type, type-swapped, or missed, so foreground-recovery and type-assignment errors are diagnosed separately. With PVT-v2-B2, SegDis attains 68.98% FG-mIoU and 78.35% mIoU on USC12K at about one-eighth of USCNet's parameters; CSCS is 5.75.

Abstract: Aligning video generation models with human preference depends critically on the quality of the reward model that scores generated videos. Current video reward models, however, source their reward signal from a model's representational rather than generative capacity: they either repurpose vision-language models as pixel-space scorers, or train auxiliary scoring heads on top of frozen generator features. The generator's actual ability to predict velocities, denoise latents, and produce videos plays no role in scoring its own generations. We argue that this overlooks a structural property of offline preference learning. Any preference optimization objective that approximates an offline preference dataset mathematically targets a strictly defined KL-constrained optimal policy. By isolating the reward term from this analytical solution and mapping the exact generation log-likelihood to its flow-matching surrogate, the empirical reward emerges deterministically as the relative difference in continuous-time velocity-prediction errors. We formalize this equivalence into GenRM-Flow, a framework where a preference-tuned video generator inherently operates as a process-aware reward model directly in the noisy latent space. We further show theoretically that distinct fine-tuning objectives, whether contrastive like DPO and IPO or non-contrastive like RWR, are alternative optimization pathways toward the same optimal policy, each inducing a reward readout from the generator's velocity-prediction errors. Furthermore, the extracted process reward seamlessly substitutes external VLM scorers in FlowAWR. Operating purely on native latent outputs without auxiliary reward networks, this generative reward drives measurable improvements in downstream video generation.

</details>

Abstract: Machine unlearning has become increasingly important in face recognition, where regulations like the GDPR empower users to request the removal of their personal data from deployed models. However, most existing unlearning methods are designed for closed-set classification with small-scale models and training data, and therefore do not generalize to real-world face recognition systems, which operate in an open-set setting with large-scale models and data. In this work, we propose Rapid Activation Dampening (RAD), a fast and targeted unlearning method tailored specifically for large-scale open-set face recognition. RAD approximates differences in average neuron activations between forget and retain identities, identifying and selectively suppressing neurons that are most associated with the targeted identity through a sensitivity-controlled dampening mechanism. We further propose the Forget Score (FS), which addresses the mismatch between existing unlearning metrics and open-set problems by providing a probabilistic interpretation of forgetting directly in the representation space. Experiments across several recent large-scale face recognition architectures and datasets show that RAD achieves strong forgetting while preserving overall model utility and substantially improving unlearning speed. These findings establish RAD as a scalable and practical approach to machine unlearning in face recognition systems.

Abstract: Agentic autoregressive video generation combines semantic planning, sequential generation, and visual verification for controllable synthesis. Growing visual histories enlarge the key-value (KV) cache, increasing memory use and attention costs across candidate rollouts. Existing compression methods primarily optimize individual generators without the planner's semantic constraints, risking low priority for states supporting requested attributes or actions. We introduce SemanticKV, a training-free method for semantically aligned KV cache compression. To our knowledge, SemanticKV is the first KV cache compression method for agentic autoregressive video generation that explicitly uses planner-extracted semantic constraints to guide retention. Text cross-attention identifies constraint-related video positions, whose self-attention queries are collected into separate groups. Attention weights are averaged within each group and maximized across groups, giving each cached entry a high score when strongly used by any represented constraint. A single top-k selection retains high-scoring entries under the memory budget, leaving their keys, values, and positions unchanged. For fixed query groups and equal entry costs, this selection minimizes an additive upper bound on worst-group discarded attention. The method operates independently within each candidate without retraining or additional video generations. We evaluate four AR video backbones on three benchmarks for 5-, 30-, and 60-second generation. On Causal Forcing at a compression ratio of 0.7, SemanticKV obtains a VBench Total Score of 84.10, compared with 84.05 for the strongest compressed baseline and 83.99 for Full KV. It achieves a 1.22x speedup over Full KV and reduces KV-cache memory from 6.04 GB to 1.81 GB, a 3.35x reduction.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic manipulation requires representations that capture both scene geometry and its evolution during interaction. Existing approaches have largely treated these two aspects separately. World action models capture such dynamics by coupling action prediction with future video generation, but their reliance on iterative diffusion or rectified-flow inference leads to high computational cost, while their representations lack explicit spatial structure. Explicitly modeling scene geometry typically requires auxiliary modules that increase both parameter count and computational cost. Conversely, geometry-centric policies primarily recover observed spatial structure and often lack explicit supervision for anticipating future geometric changes during interaction. To address these limitations, we introduce ForeAct, which repurposes a pretrained video world model as a single-pass representation backbone for low-latency control. ForeAct directly processes the clean latent of the current observation in one forward pass, eliminating future-frame synthesis and iterative denoising while retaining the backbone's pretrained spatiotemporal priors. Rather than introducing a separate geometry backbone, ForeAct uses learnable tokens supervised by next-frame relative depth to shape the shared video representation through bidirectional self-attention. This compact interface turns the spatial knowledge already encoded in the backbone into a future-aware representation for action generation, with little parameter overhead. The lightweight geometry readout is used only during training and removed at deployment, enabling low-latency, geometry-aware control without depth input or an external geometry backbone. Without additional large-scale pretraining, ForeAct achieves 96.6% average success on LIBERO, 63.5% on SimplerEnv-Bridge, and a 74.4% success rate across 90 real-world rollouts.

</details>

We propose AgentCT, a vision-language agent that identifies the degradation and routes images to task-specific restoration models. A LoRA-adapted Qwen3-VL-4B controller predicts degradation type and severity, selects a specialist, and reassesses the restored image. The LDCT specialist uses an image-domain diffusion model, whereas the SVCT and MAR specialists combine projection-domain correction, data consistency, differentiable filtered back-projection, and image refinement. Abstract: Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) asks a drone to find an object described in natural language. The description usually locates the object relative to named landmarks, such as streets and buildings. We observe that current navigators often bring the drone close to the target and then stop at the wrong place: they reach the target without finding it. This gap is shared by navigators of very different designs, from imitation learners to a fine-tuned 7B vision-language model. A simple test reveals the cause. When we replace the target object in the instruction with another one next to the same landmarks, no navigator goes to it, and most stop where they did before. Navigators learn to reach the landmarks, not to identify the described object. We therefore propose MAP-VP, a plug-and-play module for the last step. After any navigator stops, MAP-VP draws the landmarks named in the instruction onto the drone's current view, using the landmark outlines that the benchmark provides on a map. A fine-tuned 4B vision-language model then locates the target relative to these landmarks. The training data are generated automatically from the benchmark annotations. Neither drawing the landmarks nor fine-tuning helps much alone, but together they are highly effective. Without retraining any navigator, MAP-VP significantly improves all six navigators we test on CityNav. It roughly doubles the success rate of the two strongest trained navigators, raising HETT from 25.9% to 54.6% and FlightGPT from 24.2% to 48.9%, whereas CityNav's official detector-based last step adds less than two points to HETT.

To optimize the flow-matching planner online, we introduce Semantic Flow Policy Optimization (SFPO), which stochasticizes selected denoising transitions with channel-wise semantic covariance over heterogeneous HOI features and assigns group-relative, sequence-level rewards from complete closed-loop executions. Abstract: In this work, we investigate whether attention-based attribution maps in a vision transformer provide causal explanations for diabetic retinopathy (DR) classification. We define an identity relating $c_t$ and $p_t$ that characterizes when $c_t$ fails to capture causality. The pull $p_t$ achieves the highest causal alignment ($FC = 0.53$, $N = 0.71\%$, $Su = 3.0\%$), outperforming Attention Rollout, Grad-CAM, and AttnLRP. Occlusion retains better sufficiency ($Su = 1.6\%$) but is about 800 times slower. Furthermore, on MAPLES-DR, $p_t$ achieves $70\%$ of the average precision (AP) of HRDecoder, a state-of-the-art supervised lesion segmentation model, despite the absence of pixel-level supervision. Together, these findings show that the pull $p_t$ and the contribution $c_t$ form computationally efficient attribution maps for reliable causal interpretation in safety-critical medical diagnostics. Abstract: Autonomous vehicles use pedestrian crossing intention prediction (PCIP) to participate safely in traffic. Existing models are largely trained under favourable daytime conditions with sufficient daylight. Little is known about the robustness of these models under night-time and low-light conditions, due to the lack of datasets containing night-time footage. This paper investigates how a dataset, named NightSteps, can be constructed and evaluated as a baseline benchmark. The dataset is based on nighttime dashcam footage collected from six different cities around the world, capturing a wide variety of road conditions. The dataset consists of dashcam-recorded point-of-view footage, raw data of pedestrians' locations with corresponding 'track ids' and ground-truth labels containing accurate information about pedestrian crossing behaviour, resulting in a diverse dataset. Gemini, ChatGPT, and human assessment are used to demonstrate the usability of the dataset by predicting crossings in clips from the dataset and comparing the results with the initial ground-truth labels. These results show that human input achieves an accuracy of 88.17% and an F1-score of 80.21%. Predictions from Gemini and ChatGPT perform lower, with accuracies of 60% and 79.13% respectively, and F1-scores of 42.50% and 63.64%. The evaluation demonstrates that the NightSteps dataset can be used for future model evaluation to test the performance of PCIP, with the aim of improving road safety at night.

Abstract: Ray Tracing enables rendering capabilities that are difficult to achieve with rasterization-based approaches such as 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS). However, the training cost of Gaussian-based ray tracing (GRT) is much higher than that of 3DGS. In this paper, we study how ray tracing using Gaussians executes on GPUs and show that a considerable fraction of the training cost arises from how the computation is organized. We develop FastGRT, which decouples ray traversal from blending and reuses work done in the forward pass for the backward pass. FastGRT further incorporates optimizations such as fast gradient accumulation and a hybrid BVH update strategy to tackle other major bottlenecks in the training pipeline. We implement FastGRT in Vulkan. To the best of our knowledge, it is the first Vulkan-based GRT implementation that supports both training and rendering. Compared to 3DGRT, FastGRT improves training time by 1.9$\times$ to 4.97$\times$, while preserving the original rendering formulation. Our code is open source.

Yet benchmarking remains difficult, since reconstruction accuracy depends jointly on the reconstruction paradigm, object properties, and acquisition regime. Existing benchmarks often restrict viewpoint coverage, resolution, or illumination, which can bias comparisons towards methods suited to the chosen setting. We introduce Martine, a benchmark for controlled 3D object surface reconstruction. Martine comprises 28 objects, including transparent materials and fine structures, captured at 9568 x 6376 pixels from 84 views over a spherical cap under both single-light and multi-light regimes. It provides object-independent camera calibration, accurate 3D ground-truth scans, calibration and image-to-geometry accuracy quantified on test images, and 3D property masks for analysing errors by material properties, visibility, curvature and fine structures. Experiments across photogrammetry, classical multi-view stereo, neural implicit surfaces, Gaussian-splatting-based reconstruction, and multi-view photometric stereo show that Martine enables regime-aware evaluation with explicit control over reference-data accuracy. The benchmark resolves sub-millimetric reconstruction differences, with a best observed Chamfer distance of 0.058 mm over the evaluated visible surface.

Abstract: Monocular 3D detection (Mono3D) hinges on recovering depth from a single image, for which the geometric formulation $z \approx f \cdot H / h_{2D}$ is widely adopted due to its simplicity and direct grounding in projective geometry. We show that this formulation harbors a systematic, projection-induced bias: substituting the 2D bounding-box height $h_{2D}$ recovers the depth of an object's visible front surface rather than its center, causing size-dependent underestimation that persists even under ground-truth inputs. We address this front-surface bias with MonoPCA, a unified framework built on a projection-consistent reformulation. At its core, PCDepth retains the measurable $h_{2D}$ and closes the gap to center depth through an orientation- and size-aware correction derived from perspective geometry, eliminating the bias without introducing ill-posed regression targets. Because PCDepth couples depth to predicted object size and orientation, attribute perception becomes essential. We therefore introduce GeoAlign, a teacher-student framework that transfers geometric representations from Vision Foundation Models into a lightweight student, strengthening attribute perception at no inference cost. Across four challenging benchmarks, MonoPCA achieves SoTA performance, including a +1.44% AP$_{3D}$ gain on the KITTI leaderboard and +2.80% on Omni3D$_{OUT}$. Code is available at https://github.com/alanzhangcs/MonoPCA.

Abstract: Quantifying mouse behavior from pose is a foundational tool in neuroscience, ethology, drug discovery, and animal welfare. Self-supervised foundation models are a natural fit, but a foundation model for mouse behavior must handle social behavior - many behaviors that matter (aggression, courtship, mounting, allogrooming) are inherently multi-animal. Existing self-supervised pose models fall short: they encode each animal independently and discard interaction signal, hard-code the animal count, and pretrain one dataset at a time. MICE combines a hierarchical individual encoder capturing per-mouse kinematics at multiple temporal scales with a Perceiver-style social encoder whose learned latent codebook decouples parameter count from animal count, so a single checkpoint serves any group size without retraining. Across four evaluation protocols spanning six datasets, MICE matches or exceeds prior keypoint-based baselines, and layer-wise probing shows the social stage incrementally improves discriminability beyond the individual encoder. Abstract: Creating high-quality 3D content has traditionally required specialist expertise and experience with complex tools. The growing ecosystem of AI-assisted creativity demands accessible and intuitive 3D generation systems. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promising directions, yet extending their generative capabilities to 3D remains fundamentally challenging because existing 3D representations are not naturally compatible with language-based reasoning. Current approaches either depend on expensive fine-tuning or impose restrictive structural priors that sacrifice expressiveness and exclude organic shapes. We argue that the mismatch between LLM-native reasoning and conventional 3D parameterizations is a core bottleneck of LLM-driven 3D generation. To address this, we introduce the Hierarchical Patch Scaffold (HPS), a representation designed around semantically meaningful, interpretable part-level abstractions that are naturally understood by both humans and LLMs, enabling expressive, editable, and open-domain 3D generation without imposing restrictive geometric priors or expensive fine-tuning.
  Building upon HPS, we introduce SurfScaff3D, an interactive framework for text and image-to-3D generation. HPS inherits the flexibility of B-spline patches, while exposing an interface that leverages open-world capabilities of foundation models to reliably generate 3D. Its hierarchical part-level semantic labels enable targeted, natural-language edits at any level of granularity without re-running generation. Further, HPS offers explicit per-patch control over surface detail, enabling more expressive geometry capable of representing arbitrary objects. Extensive experiments against state-of-the-art LLM-based 3D generation methods demonstrate strong geometric fidelity, semantic alignment, and flexible editing across diverse object categories.

However, existing public datasets offer limited scalability and diversity in real-world clinical scenarios. Moreover, annotations typically focus on either tissue or instruments alone, overlooking tool-tissue interactions. This pipeline adapts foundation priors to surgical videos and produces temporally consistent trajectories, pseudo-scale geometry, camera poses, and instrument masks. The dataset captures realistic challenges inherent to surgical environments, including occlusions, smoke, bleeding, and rapid instrument motion. We establish a comprehensive benchmark on SurgPose, SurgT, and STIR, covering both instrument and tissue tracking. We demonstrate instrument displacement estimation, deformable surface reconstruction, and foundation-model fine-tuning. Extensive experiments and analysis demonstrate the effectiveness of our dataset and annotation framework for 3D surgical point tracking, reconstruction, and surgical action understanding. Abstract: Detection calibration aligns predicted confidence with empirical correctness. Existing calibration losses inherit their correctness definition from box regression, specifically IoU against a hard threshold. We show that this target is geometrically ill-posed for tiny objects in UAV infrared imagery, where persons occupy less than 0.1\% of the image. The same pixel error is penalized more heavily as the target shrinks, so binary prediction calibration (BPC) actively suppresses confidences that should be high. On HIT-UAV, BPC with an IoU target degrades image-level localization-aware ECE (LaECE) from 0.0689 to 0.0761, 0.0649 to 0.0733, and 0.0653 to 0.0682 across Low, Mid, and High signal-to-clutter ratio (SCR) groups. We replace the IoU target with a continuous, scale-invariant normalized Wasserstein distance (NWD). The NWD target reduces LaECE\(_{\mathrm{NWD}}\) relative to BPC+IoU on all three groups (\(p<10^{-7}\)) with mAP neutral (0.9356 vs.\ 0.9347), and an independent center-offset anchor confirms that the gain is a genuine geometric correction. The improvement holds across a broad plateau of calibration weights. The NWD formulation requires no inference-time mapping layer. If a validation-fitted isotonic map is applied on top of it, calibration is lowered further, and the two corrections act on different components of the same error. The metric-level remedy has a measurable boundary. Target-background thermal pairing in the training distribution and detection-level aggregation under per-image detection-count heterogeneity lie outside it. Both are quantified independently of the NWD target.

</details>

Abstract: Optical flow requires reliable matching from sufficient visual context and precise motion at every pixel. Existing methods such as WAFT match and estimate flow on the same coarse grids, then recover dense flow through learned upsampling, without explicitly linking visual support for matching to dense flow reconstruction. Our key insight is that dense pixel-level flow does not require equally dense matching. We decouple correspondence scale from flow resolution by adapting the size and shape of spatial supports to image structure for reliable matching, while reusing these supports for precise dense flow reconstruction. We realize this in Flow from Adaptive Token Estimation (FATE), whose token supports expand within coherent regions and contract near motion boundaries. Shared pixel-to-token associations pool features for matching and reconstruct pixel flow from token motions. Flow supervision jointly trains pixel grouping, token matching, and flow estimation without grouping labels, allowing FATE to anticipate which pixels are likely to move together from appearance alone. Trained on FlyingChairs/FlyingThings3D, FATE achieves the highest zero-shot accuracy on Sintel Final and KITTI, a critical setting given scarce real-world flow labels. For visible pixels, FATE improves accuracy within coherent regions, and more near motion boundaries. Its largest gains occur where direct matches are unavailable: Occluded or out-of-frame pixels account for roughly 10% of valid pixels but 50% of WAFT's total error. Experiments show that adaptive correspondence through pixel-to-token grouping enables reliable matching and precise pixel-level flow, improving generalization to unseen real scenes.

</details>

Abstract: Compact object detectors such as YOLOv8n are a common choice for camera-based fire and smoke detection on low-power devices. These detectors are usually evaluated with box-level metrics such as mean average precision, and their outputs are turned into decisions with a fixed confidence threshold such as 0.25 or 0.5. We compare three simple rules for turning box detections into one alarm score per image, and we use a split conformal procedure to choose the alarm threshold from held-out images so that the expected fraction of missed fire and smoke images stays below a chosen target. We find that the same fixed threshold leads to very different miss rates for the two detectors, 17.3 and 8.4 percent at a threshold of 0.5, while the calibrated threshold meets miss-rate targets of 10, 5 and 1 percent for both detectors and all three rules. At a 5 percent target, the false alarm rate on images without fire or smoke stays between 1.2 and 3.0 percent. Abstract: Monocular depth estimation has advanced through large-scale pretraining and strong visual backbones, yet metric depth remains sensitive to scene-level scale. Indoor rooms and outdoor driving scenes occupy very different depth ranges, but most depth decoders infer this global structure implicitly from visual features. We ask whether scene-level semantic structure can instead act as an upstream prior that shapes the metric output space before dense prediction.
  We present LangDepth (SemanticBins), a monocular depth architecture that couples strong visual features with a scene-conditioned output space. A frozen CLIP encoder and a learnable bank of scene- and geometry-oriented concept keys convert image features into a compact scene descriptor, without requiring per-image text or an external captioner. A Scene Prior Module (SPM) maps this descriptor to a scene-conditioned metric depth interval, providing a global range anchor before dense decoding. In parallel, a DINOv2-L backbone with a lightweight FPN extracts dense visual features, and a Per-Pixel Assignment Network (PPAN) assigns pixels to the scene-prescribed bin structure. A confidence gate fuses the dense visual estimate with the SPM prior.
  We train LangDepth jointly on NYU Depth V2 and KITTI under a modest supervised depth budget, without web-scale depth supervision or pseudo-labelled depth data. A single model achieves delta_1 = 0.960 on NYU, 0.960 on KITTI, 0.887 on DIODE indoor, 0.946 on iBims-1, and 0.861 on SUN RGB-D. Beyond aggregate accuracy, the SPM predicts compact indoor ranges and much wider outdoor KITTI ranges, the gate becomes SPM-dominant on outdoor scenes, and perturbing the routed scene context causes large drops on KITTI and iBims-1. These results support scene-conditioned output spaces as a practical and interpretable mechanism for cross-domain monocular depth estimation.

Abstract: Alignment of text-to-image diffusion models with reward functions is a key challenge in controllable generation. Existing approaches typically rely on optimizing model inputs or fine-tuning denoising networks, which requires computationally expensive backpropagation through large denoising models. In this work, we propose Direct Trajectory Optimization (DTO), a training-free framework that performs alignment by directly optimizing the sampling trajectory of diffusion models. Specifically, DTO treats intermediate denoising scores and sampled noise as the optimization variables, eliminating the need for gradients through the denoising network. To mitigate the out-of-distribution problem, we introduce Reward-Optimized Score Guidance, which interpolates between the optimized scores and the pretrained model predictions to stabilize sampling, while providing a provable theoretical guarantee of reward improvement under constrained distributions. Empirically, DTO improves alignment performance across three diffusion models on two benchmarks, outperforming existing inference-time alignment methods while maintaining high efficiency. These results suggest that trajectory-level optimization is a promising direction for efficient and effective diffusion model alignment.

Abstract: When a vision-language model encounters a new class or domain, how much should it trust its learned prompts? Because source adaptation can correct pretrained errors on some target images while disturbing valid predictions on others, we introduce Contextual Adaptive Fusion (CAF), which retains four prediction branches by pairing frozen and prompted visual and textual representations. To arbitrate when branches disagree on an image, CAF combines each branch's predictive confidence with how frequently the branches favor its proposed class across the unlabeled target collection, placing greater trust in predictions that are locally confident yet less dominated by collection-wide frequency bias. The fusion coefficients are learned once on source data and remain fixed at inference. Trained on ImageNet, CAF achieves 69.96% average accuracy across ten cross-dataset benchmarks (1.92 percentage points above ManiPT) and remains highly competitive across four ImageNet variants, setting a new best of 52.21% on ImageNet-S. Existing quality enhancement methods typically apply super-resolution (SR) or compression artifact removal (CAR) independently or in a sequential manner and are largely optimized for human visual fidelity rather than machine perception. These results demonstrate that joint optimization is critical for compression-aware industrial machine vision systems.

Abstract: Label-space expansion - predicting a larger joint space and summing back to the original labels - is a natural design for multi-label facial expression recognition, but its failure modes have not been characterised. The central finding is that the aggregation rule, not the representation, decides whether the method works. With a fixed threshold of 0.5, the aggregate score spans 28x across five backbones at 10% of the training data (0.24 to 6.79) and collapses to a 1.9x range at full data, while threshold-free mAP stays within 1.28x over the same backbones, which localises the fault to the summation step. The mechanism is the uncalibrated aggregation rule, which adds the main-branch score to half the sum of the three non-main branches; we verify it by measuring main- and non-main-branch activations. Two plain seven-dimensional sigmoid baselines (BCE, ASL) reach mAP 0.5055 and 0.5175; ASL exceeds three of the five CALE configurations and falls within 0.03 of the best. On single-label FER the gain over a seven-dimensional baseline is nominally significant (paired t-test, p = 0.0436) but does not survive Benjamini-Hochberg correction (p_BH = 0.2055) or a Wilcoxon test (p = 0.1250). We conclude that sum-based label expansion requires threshold calibration at the deployment data scale, or a learned aggregation, before it can support multi-label decisions.

Abstract: Attention heads in video Transformers vary greatly in functional importance. Understanding their contributions requires examining not only which heads are important, but also how they participate in collective computation within the network. We study this collective computation from two perspectives: the downstream use of head outputs and the composition of interactions across layers. Ablation analyses of four video Transformers show that high-importance heads identified on a single task also support other video understanding tasks and help preserve the representational structure that distinguishes different videos. Representational responses to head ablation correlate weakly with head importance after the attention module, but more strongly after the MLP in the same block. Adapting this downstream processing through MLP-only fine-tuning further improves the use of lower-importance head outputs. Meanwhile, through composition across layers, sets of heads with shorter spatiotemporal attention distances support stronger perturbation responses at distant tokens than sets with longer distances. Their joint ablation causes even greater performance loss than ablating equally sized sets of high-importance heads, revealing collective contributions not fully captured by individual importance rankings. These findings highlight collective computation as a key perspective for understanding attention head function: functional contributions depend on both how head outputs are used downstream and how local interactions compose across layers.

</details>

In vacancy classification, filtering and alignment raised AUROC from 0.684 to 0.719, but marking a different house in the same image still gave 0.709. Grad-CAM showed that the model often looked at areas outside the target house. Image suitability, target identification, and model evidence are therefore separate problems, and scores alone do not resolve them. Promptable models such as SEEM (Segment Everything Everywhere All at Once) allow click-based refinement, but without adaptation SEEM segments thin vessels poorly and needs many clicks. Yet SEEM reads every candidate mask from one feature map at quarter resolution, too coarse for vessels one to three pixels wide. This study introduces the Read-out Basis Adapter (RBA), whose read-out branch generates 32 image-specific basis maps at half resolution. Each candidate accesses them through its own vector and a zero-initialised projection, so the model starts exactly as SEEM. The encoder branch, a standard rank-1 LoRA, supplies the information needed to tell arteries from veins. RBA adds 0.47 million trainable parameters. Under simulated error-driven clicks, with the ground truth used only to place them, RBA outperforms LoRA, Conv-LoRA andAdaptFormer on OCTA-500 3mmlarge vessels. Averaged over three seeds, it reaches an intersection over union (IoU) of 85.16% and a Dice score of 91.93%, 2.90 and 1.72 percentage points above Conv-LoRA. On the 6 mm subset, one run gives margins of 4.35 and 2.76 points. The gain extends to arteries, veins and ROSE-O but not to the FAZ. In SEEM, adaptation capacity pays more at the mask read-out than in the encoder.

Abstract: The wide field of view of fisheye cameras and the simplicity of a monocular setup make monocular fisheye visual odometry (VO) attractive for robotic localization and mapping. Nevertheless, scale drift remains a major challenge over long trajectories. Unsupervised depth learning can help to mitigate this issue, but the pronounced distortion of fisheye images poses challenges for accurate depth estimation. This article presents S$^2$D-FVO, a monocular fisheye VO framework integrating spherical bundle adjustment (SBA)-guided unsupervised depth learning to improve scale consistency and depth estimation accuracy. Given that pixel reprojection residuals do not uniformly reflect angular deviations between viewing rays and that the first-order linearization of a scalar angular reprojection residual is insensitive to perturbations along one tangent direction, SBA employs tangent-space angular reprojection residuals derived from the spherical logarithmic map. These residuals retain sensitivity along both tangent directions, with their norms equal to the corresponding scalar angular residuals. A median depth-based scale regularization term is further integrated into SBA to constrain the reconstruction scale. Building on these formulations, SBA replaces the pose estimation network during unsupervised depth learning, providing more reliable relative poses for view synthesis. In turn, depth estimates from the well-trained depth estimation network provide scale references for SBA, improving scale consistency while allowing individual 3-D landmark positions to be refined. Extensive experiments conducted on multiple public datasets, as well as two newly collected large-scale datasets, demonstrate that the proposed framework outperforms representative existing approaches in both depth and trajectory estimation accuracy and is applicable to different camera models.

Abstract: Video reasoning hinges on a small number of visually decisive tokens embedded in otherwise largely language-driven chains of thought. Yet prevailing post-training methods such as GRPO overlook this structure, assigning coarse trajectory-level rewards while requiring multiple rollouts and often costly external judges. We introduce Visually-Aware On-Policy Self-Distillation (VIOS), a post-training framework that identifies where a model under-utilizes visual evidence and concentrates learning at those positions. Our key insight is that a model can audit its own visual reliance: the divergence between its next-token distributions with and without the video reveals how strongly each prediction depends on the visual input. Measuring this reliance for both a student and an answer-privileged teacher exposes a per-token reliance gap, identifying positions where the teacher exploits visual evidence that the student overlooks. VIOS translates this signal into dense, targeted supervision through three complementary components: an exponential moving average (EMA) teacher that co-evolves with the student, a contrastive objective that encourages predictions to depend on the video, and a reliance-gap gate that focuses learning on visually under-utilized tokens. Crucially, VIOS requires only a single on-policy rollout from a single model, without external rewards, judges, or larger teachers. Across 12 benchmarks spanning general, temporal, spatial, knowledge-intensive, and long-video understanding, VIOS consistently improves three model backbones and outperforms contemporary video-reasoning systems, while training in over 9x fewer steps, 60x fewer FLOPs, and 111x fewer generated tokens. Project page: https://vision.cs.utexas.edu/projects/VIOS/

Abstract: We present UltraVoxelGS, a 3D ultrasound reconstruction method that achieves both fast reconstruction and physically faithful rendering for the first time, bringing live spatial feedback during 2D ultrasound scanning within reach. This is made possible by a feed-forward design that predicts a complete Gaussian scene representation from posed ultrasound images directly, entirely bypassing the per-scene optimization bottleneck that has confined prior methods to offline use. Realising such feed-forward prediction in this setting is non-trivial: unlike natural images, ultrasound slices share at most one-dimensional intersections and exhibit view-dependent appearance due to acoustic propagation and attenuation, violating the dense overlap and photometric consistency assumed by existing feed-forward Gaussian methods. Hence, to achieve fast reconstruction, we introduce a feed-forward voxel-to-Gaussian pass that decouples representation size from input count by lifting all posed slices into a fixed-size voxel support and predicting Gaussian parameters directly. For faithful rendering, we propose ultrasound-aware appearance rendering strategy factorize reflection from cumulative attenuation during training and exploit appearance continuity among spatially adjacent slices for efficient Render-time Appearance Adaptation. On both real-world and simulated datasets, UltraVoxelGS achieves best PSNR/SSIM compared with per-scene optimization baselines while reducing inference from 10-20 minutes to approximately 10 seconds (over 35 times faster) and scales to sequences exceeding 1,000 frames. Code is available at https://github.com/YuebyYue/UltraVoxelGS-camera-ready.

In this work, we formulate adaptation of 3D reconstruction models as a problem of correspondence recovery under domain shift and propose 3R-Adapter, a PEFT paradigm for long-tail 3D reconstruction scenarios. Our method consists of three components: Deformable Retrieval for task-adaptive relation candidate retrieval, Relational Rewiring for reconstructing cross-view relational structures, and Consistency Refinement for injecting the rewired relations into stable predictions. Extensive experiments on low-light reconstruction, joint human-scene reconstruction, and test-time adaptation demonstrate that our approach significantly improves robustness over existing PEFT methods while maintaining efficient lightweight adaptation. Our code is released at https://github.com/PKU-OV3-LAB/3R-Adapter.

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in image generation and editing, yet this progress has also introduced significant security risks. In this paper, we propose AnyBreak, a novel jailbreak framework that constructs adversarial images via multi-scale progressive semantic alignment. During the generation process, these adversarial images steer the generation trajectory in latent space towards unsafe data manifolds, enabling cross-modal transfer of malicious concepts from the text modality to the image modality. AnyBreak operates using only clean textual prompts and adversarial images at inference time, and can jailbreak any T2I models towards any malicious concepts in a fully black-box setting. We further introduce AnyBreak-V, an enhanced variant that incorporates a compensation term to bypass post-hoc safety checkers. Extensive experiments demonstrate that AnyBreak achieves high attack success rates while preserving visual fidelity and semantic consistency with the input prompts under black-box and clean-text constraints. Abstract: Streaming 3D reconstruction with recurrent states enables long-video scene understanding under bounded memory, but turns reconstruction into a state-evolution problem: each incoming frame updates a compact scene memory on which all subsequent predictions depend, so unreliable updates overwrite historical context and accumulate into long-horizon geometric drift. We analyze CUT3R-style recurrent updates through a direction--magnitude decomposition, in which the magnitude sets the update gain, i.e., how strongly an observation is written into the state, and the direction encodes the geometric intent, i.e., where the update drives the state. This decomposition provides a unified view of CUT3R and its variants, revealing that existing training-free extensions perform magnitude-only control: they can attenuate an unreliable update but cannot correct its direction. We propose HIST3R, a training-free framework built on CUT3R that controls both components using history. HIST3R rectifies the update direction using locally consistent historical states, and modulates the update magnitude according to the visual discrepancy between the current observation and its history, suppressing redundant updates while preserving complementary evidence. Across camera pose estimation, 3D reconstruction, and video depth estimation, HIST3R consistently improves long-horizon performance while retaining the memory footprint and inference speed of CUT3R. With 1000 input frames, it reduces ATE by 23.87% on ScanNet and 33.65% on TUM-Dynamics relative to the strongest competing method; with 500 frames, it improves median reconstruction accuracy over the strongest CUT3R-based variant by 56.52% on 7-Scenes and 33.96% on NRGBD.

</details>

Abstract: Recent anomaly detection studies have shown that residual features, which model the discrepancy between normal and abnormal patterns, provide strong generalization to anomalies unseen during training. However, existing residual-based approaches mainly rely on visual discrepancies derived from image representations, while semantic discrepancies that can be explicitly expressed through language remain largely underexplored. To address this problem, we propose TIRAD, Text-guided Image Residual Learning for Generalist Anomaly Detection, a multimodal residual learning framework that defines the discrepancy between textual representations of normal and abnormal states as a Text Residual Feature and uses it to guide the Image Residual Feature. Specifically, a Text Fusion Module converts patch-level text residuals into a gate that modulates the image residual of each patch. The gate retains the visual discrepancies that the text residual identifies as defect-related and attenuates the others, leading to more discriminative anomaly representations. Extensive experiments on industrial benchmarks and industrial-to-medical transfer demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing image-centric residual learning methods.

Abstract: Cross-view image sequence geo-localization aims to determine the geographic location of a sequence of ground images by matching it against geo-tagged satellite imagery. Although sequential observations provide complementary scene information, substantial overlap between adjacent images introduces feature redundancy, while conventional fusion strategies may weaken spatial relationships essential for cross-view matching. Therefore, we propose a cross-view image sequence geo-localization method via Attention-guided Sequence fusion and Metric Learning (ASML). Specifically, a Sequential Content Fusion (SCF) module models cross-image feature correlations to suppress redundant content and adaptively preserve geographically informative non-overlapping regions. Next, a Spatial Feature Aggregation (SFA) module is used to capture and aggregate the spatial and semantic information of local features, so as to maintain the spatial layout of the image sequence. Finally, a Geo-InfoNCE loss function is proposed, which comprehensively considers the feature distance and geographic distance for mixed-space metric learning, thus improving the ability of the model to distinguish hard negative samples. A new cross-view image sequence dataset CrossRoad-66K is established to provide more complex scenarios. Extensive experiments and visualizations on the SeqGeo and CrossRoad-66K datasets demonstrate that compared to state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods, the proposed method ASML achieves an improvement of 3.37% and 2.10% at R@1, respectively.

Abstract: Can a large language model (LLM) write the parameters of a medical image classifier from medical knowledge alone, without any training data? Such classifiers normally require labelled images, which are costly and often unavailable. We investigate this question through weight-space programming (WSP), in which an LLM writes or adapts the weights and biases of a neural network directly, without optimisation on training data. Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra each generate the parameters of a small LeNet-style CNN in a single reply, without tool use and without access to any image. We compare these networks against zero-shot MedSigLIP and against the same architecture trained on labels using the Adam optimiser, across 7 classification tasks in four modalities: chest X-ray, breast ultrasound and abdominal CT (all from MedMNIST), and pelvic MRI (SynthUterus). The LLM-written networks perform above chance on all six MedMNIST tasks, with a mean test AUC of 0.719 for Opus and 0.711 for Astra. Both models outperform MedSigLIP on 5 of 6 tasks while using more than 10,000 times fewer parameters. However, the same network trained with Adam on only 256 labelled images achieves a higher AUC on every MedMNIST task (mean AUC 0.828). On SynthUterus, Opus attains an AUC of 0.764, whereas Astra falls below chance (AUC 0.136) owing to a systematic error. These results show that LLMs can write functional medical classifiers, but that they do not yet replace training data. We release the protocol, logs and all generated weights at https://github.com/MaximilianLindholz/weight-space-programming-medical.

</details>

Abstract: Video diffusion transformers (DiTs) generate realistic videos, but their heavy computational cost limits broader real-world applications. Mixed-resolution generation reduces this cost by generating the visually important region at high resolution (HR) and the rest at low resolution (LR), yet existing methods suffer a severe quality drop on videos and require knowing the HR region in advance. We show that these failures are not inherent to mixed resolution, and propose $\textbf{MPEG-Diffusion}$ to address their three root causes: (1) dual-resolution attention to mitigate the curse of mixed resolution, where each query reads the K/V of its own resolution, (2) a mixed-resolution decoder to blend the HR and LR latents before decoding and resolve distortion, and (3) a self-selected HR region using the DiT's knowledge to find the visually important region without requiring it in advance. Processing only $30\%$ of full-resolution tokens, MPEG-Diffusion speeds up denoising by $4.8\times$ on Wan2.1 14B ($3.8\times$ on 1.3B), outperforming prior methods on nearly every metric. In the user study, we demonstrate the high quality of MPEG-Diffusion, with raters preferring it over prior mixed-resolution methods in over $90\%$ of pairs and rating it close to full resolution.

</details>

Abstract: Remote face authentication requires protection against both presentation attacks and biometric data injection attacks prior to face matching. Methods based on active screen illumination appear promising for both by introducing randomized visual stimuli through the user's device screen and evaluating the corresponding facial responses. Although such methods have been more studied for presentation attack detection (PAD), their systematic evaluation for injection attack detection (IAD) remains limited. To address this gap, we first classify injected content into static images, prerecorded videos, and real-time deepfakes based on their expected responses to the challenge. We then adapt an active screen-illumination approach into an implementation we term color wave and develop a systematic, injection-specific evaluation protocol. The protocol examines detection effectiveness, generalization across operating conditions, robustness to signal degradation, and susceptibility to selected evasion strategies. The findings demonstrate a strong discriminative signal under controlled conditions but also reveal performance limitations under overexposure, low screen brightness, and subject motion, as well as vulnerabilities to selected evasion strategies. By relating detection outcomes to attack types and operating conditions, this study provides an interpretable assessment of when and why active screen illumination succeeds or fails as an IAD mechanism.

Abstract: Deep learning models, particularly convolutional neural networks (CNNs), are widely adopted in computer vision, yet their opaque decision-making raises concerns in high-risk applications like healthcare and autonomous systems, where trust and accountability are critical. Therefore, robust explainable AI (XAI) methods are desirable to provide transparent insights into model decisions. To ensure these methods are reliable and effective across diverse scenarios, it is important to understand how model performance and architectural complexity influence explanation quality. Our study takes an initial step toward systematically investigating the impact of these two factors on key XAI evaluation metrics. We constructed a pipeline using 10 subsets of ImageNet-1K, each containing 80 unique classes, on each of which we trained three CNN architectures of increasing structural complexity: VGG16, ResNet50, and ConvNeXt-Tiny. For each individual model, we analyzed 10 training checkpoints representing increasing levels of model accuracy using two XAI methods: Grad-CAM (feature-attribution-based) and CRAFT (concept-based). We assessed these methods across four evaluation categories: Faithfulness, Correctness, Sensitivity, and Simplicity. Our findings reveal that as training progresses, the complex architectures (ResNet50 and ConvNeXt-Tiny) exhibit improved Faithfulness and lower Sensitivity, indicating more robust explanations. In contrast, VGG16's simpler architecture yields simpler but less faithful and correct explanations.

Abstract: Camera-LiDAR calibration is essential for reliable perception in robotic and autonomous systems. Most target-based calibration methods rely on checkerboard observations, but accurate checkerboard feature extraction from LiDAR point clouds remains challenging due to measurement sparsity. Existing approaches either require manual annotation of 3D corners or depend on geometric feature extraction methods, both of which can become unreliable when checkerboard features are insufficiently observed. In this paper, we formulate checkerboard localization as a 2D detection problem using cylindrical LiDAR reflectivity images. To overcome the limited availability of labeled training data, we propose ray-aware physics augmentation (RAPA), which generates diverse synthetic samples from a single checkerboard observation. The generated data are used to improve detector robustness and generalization under varying sensing conditions. Across four independently captured indoor and outdoor locations, the proposed synthetic augmentation exceeds the strongest evaluated image-space augmentation baseline by 16.1-43.1 percentage points in mAP@0.5. Compared with training-free target-based methods on 242 observations, RAPA yields the largest number of valid target localizations, with 96.4% of its returned detections yielding valid target poses. These reliable localizations support downstream camera-LiDAR calibration across all four evaluated locations. Overall, RAPA addresses a key bottleneck in target-based calibration by enabling automated LiDAR-side target localization with only a single annotated target observation.

It contains approximately 1.2 million images from 142 countries, paired with 25 million structured annotations covering scene captions, incident and location assessments, spatial relationships, and visual question answering (VQA). The VQA annotations link answers to visible evidence and include questions about both present and absent cues, multiple answer formats, and difficulty labels. Abstract: Evaluating generative models requires measuring not only sample fidelity but also how well a generated set covers the real data distribution. Recent work has therefore introduced recall-oriented metrics for distribution-level evaluation. However, existing recall metrics often reduce distributional coverage to geometry-based coverage. This simplifying assumption weakens their diagnostic value and interpretability in two ways: these metrics provide only a single aggregate score that cannot distinguish essential diversity failures such as mode dropping and mode collapse, and geometry-based coverage can provide unreliable estimates by assigning high scores to poorly covered generative distributions. We therefore introduce CADMA, a capacity-aware framework for recall-oriented evaluation and diagnosis. CADMA evaluates distributional coverage through local capacity matching between real and generated samples, separating geometry-based coverage from effective coverage. This yields multi-dimensional coverage diagnostics that quantify mode dropping and mode collapse while reducing the overly optimistic estimates produced by geometry-based metrics. Through extensive experiments on toy data, real-world datasets, and generative models, we show that CADMA provides more reliable coverage estimates and more fine-grained, interpretable diagnostic signals of diversity failures than existing metrics. Our code is available at https://github.com/Open-Debin/CADMA_NeurIPS2026.

The framework provides a reproducible basis for evaluating future egocentric foundation models in specialised forensic settings.

Abstract: Vision-language models such as CLIP achieve strong performance across computer vision tasks, yet their image-text alignment is less reliable for infrared (IR) imagery because large-scale pre-training is dominated by visible-spectrum data. On four source-disjoint IR classification benchmarks, TMT achieves the highest mean zero-shot accuracy among the compared methods (48.92%). Abstract: Multimodal retrieval increasingly supports agentic systems, where user requests can involve cross-level relations, combine multiple constraints, and evolve across dialogue turns. In these settings, a highly similar result may still violate the user's requirements, while retrieved content may directly inform downstream actions without human screening. We introduce TRIAD, a multimodal retrieval benchmark for evaluating how well retrieval systems satisfy these complex, evolving requests. TRIAD comprises three tracks: abstraction, testing bidirectional retrieval between visual instances and concepts at different hierarchical levels; constraints, testing the joint satisfaction of positive and negative conditions; and state, testing the integration of requirements across dialogue turns and the updating of earlier conditions when users revise their requests. The benchmark contains six subsets, each with 1,000 queries and a fixed pool of 1,000 candidates per query, evaluated using a common multi-positive mean average precision (mAP) protocol. Simpler reference tasks and hard negatives grouped by the requirements they violate help characterize specific retrieval difficulties. We evaluate 20 configurations of 17 systems and find that no system leads across all six subsets. Performance declines with hierarchical distance, and negative-only queries generally underperform positive-only queries with the same number of constraints. Across paired tasks expressing the same final intent, multi-turn retrieval yields lower mAP in 39 of 40 configuration-domain comparisons. The benefits of explicit reasoning also vary across models and tasks. These findings highlight the gap between retrieving similar content and satisfying a complete request.

Abstract: An edge video analytics system must estimate the quality of local detections since there are no ground-truth labels available during runtime. Detectors usually provide confidence scores for their results, but these scores can not reveal every missed object or misplaced bounding box. So a quality estimator is necessary to provide a decision signal indicating which frame has potential detection failures that need to be escalated to a more powerful model in the cloud. Neural network based estimators add workload to the GPU already running detection, while CPU capacity may remain available. We present Predictive Temporal Consistency IoU (PTC-IoU), which moves this workload to CPU by compacting detection boxes histories to support deterministic result scoring. Comparing to neural counterparts, experiments on representative edge hardware, Nvidia Jetson Orin Nano, show that our method reduced per-frame estimator latency from 10.3 ms for neural counterparts to 2.2 ms (4.7x faster), increased pipelined throughput by 13.6%, and cuts estimator energy consumption by up to 95%, while maintaining competitive final detection quality verified on multiple datasets.

Abstract: While modern video generative models achieve remarkable visual fidelity, they remain noticeably brittle when generating physically plausible dynamics and complex interactions. Targeted post-training is crucial, yet supervised fine-tuning suffers from offline state mismatch, and reinforcement learning is hindered by sparse, ill-defined physical reward functions. On-Policy (Self-)Distillation (OPSD) reconciles on-policy rollouts with dense corrective feedback, shifting the core challenge to designing effective privileged context. However, conventional modalities fall short: text prompts are inherently declarative, whereas real reference videos heavily entangle physical laws with visual appearance without parametric control. To this end, we propose PhysOPSD, introducing executable code as privileged physical context for video generation. Unlike static visual observations, executable code instantiates an editable physical world with intrinsic abstraction and controllability. Powered by PhysOPSD-Engine, we translate real-world physical scenarios into verifiable simulation programs. PhysOPSD then incorporates (i) dual-level physical privilege to bridge the information gap via an intermediate bottleneck, alongside (ii) controlled physical variations under shared rollout noise to expose causal dynamic shifts. Extensive experiments demonstrate that PhysOPSD substantially improves physical plausibility across diverse backbones, outperforming SFT and RL baselines with only $3$K samples, while preserving general video generation quality.

</details>

Abstract: Landslide susceptibility models are trained on inventories that record where landslides were seen, not where they occurred. The established remedy fits bias-describing variables and neutralises them when mapping. We adapt occupancy-detection modelling from ecology, treating several inventories as imperfect observers of one event and separating occurrence from recording, and ask when this improves on neutralisation. We use near-complete inventories of the 2018 Hokkaido Eastern Iburi, 2015 Gorkha and 2010 Haiti earthquakes with simulated reporting bias, and three real Gorkha inventories. Compared with standard practice, the occupancy model reduces road bias in 24 of 25 paired draws and raises AUC by 0.08-0.14 on Gorkha and 0.03 on Haiti, but only 0.01-0.02 on Iburi. Its median predicted-to-true landslide rate is 0.99 (range 0.44-1.89), against 0.24-0.30 for the baselines. Rescaling the baselines with the same anchor area corrects their median but leaves larger errors (range 0.20-4.34). Cell-level probabilities remain too extreme (calibration slope 0.59). Compared with the best neutralised model, AUC is higher on Iburi (by 0.03-0.07) and lower on Gorkha and Haiti (by 0.01-0.05). Transplant experiments tie the Iburi advantage to the structure of its true landslide pattern, not to its terrain or road network. In all eight settings with a low interaction gap, computable from biased records, neutralisation was at least as accurate, including a prespecified test on Haiti; a high gap did not guarantee an advantage. The real Gorkha inventories are slope-biased, not road-biased, and there the model brings no meaningful gain. Report-based catalogues remain the main untested case.

Abstract: Generalizable deepfake video detection remains challenging because forgery artifacts vary substantially across manipulation types and often combine diverse spatial and temporal inconsistencies. Existing parameter-efficient adaptation methods typically rely on a single shared representation, rendering them prone to manipulation-specific bias and limiting transfer to unseen forgeries. To address this challenge, we propose Mixture Of Spatio-Temporal Experts (MoSTE), an adaptation framework that enables conditional specialization of forensic representations. Instead of forcing one adapter to model all forgery patterns, MoSTE decomposes adaptation into parallel spatial and temporal expert pools and dynamically routes tokens to the most relevant experts through an unsupervised token-level routing mechanism. This allows experts to naturally specialize in complementary forgery cues without requiring explicit manipulation labels or multi-stage training, while effectively transferring representations from CLIP to the video domain. Extensive experiments across cross-dataset and cross-manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that MoSTE sets a new state of the art in average generalization while preserving strong performance across diverse manipulation families. Additional analysis of expert routing and attention behavior reveals the emergence of expert diversity, suggesting that MoSTE learns complementary spatio-temporal forensic features. Code is available at https://github.com/charbelyah/MoSTE

Abstract: Video out-painting is an important problem, yet existing research focuses primarily on short clips, leaving long video out-painting largely underexplored. Extending video out-painting to long videos poses substantial challenges. A natural solution is iterative out-painting, i.e., generating videos chunk by chunk, but this introduces two major issues: generation instability or even failure caused by inter-chunk inconsistencies and error accumulation, and semantic drift over long temporal horizons. In this paper, we propose LongSCOP, short for Semantically Consistent long Video Out-Painting. First, we introduce a training recipe with three components for smooth and stable long-horizon generation: self-forcing training, latent calibration, and future references. Beyond generation stability, we recognize that the rich temporal context of long videos provides valuable references for maintaining semantic consistency. We therefore develop a VLM-based agent that automatically selects the most informative reference frames from the entire video to maintain consistent subjects, scenes, and backgrounds throughout long-horizon generation. We also construct a high-quality training dataset of 62K video clips. To facilitate research on this underexplored problem, we introduce LVO-Bench, a benchmark curated by human experts to assess long-horizon stability and semantic consistency. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method outperforms existing approaches in both generation stability and semantic coherence.

</details>

Abstract: Multi-view anomaly detection is crucial for industrial inspection to identify defects hidden from single viewpoints. Yet, despite their strong success in single-view applications, reconstruction-based methods remain largely underexplored in multi-view settings. A key challenge in extending these methods lies in generating view-consistent pseudo-anomalies. To address this, we propose PAR, a reconstruction-based framework for enhanced multi-view anomaly detection. First, we introduce a feature perturbation strategy coupled with a consensus aggregation module that integrates these perturbed features across viewpoints to mitigate view-inconsistent anomalies and construct highly aligned pseudo-anomaly representations. Second, we employ a view-specialized multi-decoder architecture; by assigning a dedicated decoder to each view, we increase the model's representational complexity, enabling the generation of highly feasible, localized anomaly maps rather than less precise, view-agnostic reconstructions. Last, we incorporate Fourier attention into the decoders to emphasize global frequency-domain structures over local token evidence, effectively preventing identity mapping. Extensive experiments on the Real-IAD dataset demonstrate that PAR consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods in sample-level, image-level, and pixel-level anomaly detection. Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used as automated judges for image-editing benchmarks, yet their reliability is poorly understood. In this paper, we introduce EditJudge-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,382 synthetic before/after image pairs rendered in Blender, with edits parameterised across nine categories derived from real user requests and exact ground truth from known 3D scene parameters. Abstract: Video world models conditioned on an input video make it possible to explore real, recorded dynamic scenes, enabling re-shooting of footage, free-viewpoint replay, and real-to-sim environments for embodied agents. Such uses require the generated rollout to stay faithful to the recorded world, yet existing benchmarks start from a single image or a text prompt, and video-conditioned generation is evaluated mainly on perceptual quality and camera following. These evaluations do not directly assess whether exploration preserves subject identity, event progression, and scene consistency upon revisiting a location. We introduce WanderBench, a benchmark for exploring dynamic worlds from video. It comprises 745 single-shot source videos of real and AI-generated content in three difficulty tiers (3-25 s), paired with camera routes given as keyboard actions, and evaluates six dimensions with 24 human-validated sub-metrics. Benchmarking eight models, we find that as clips grow longer, models keep the static scene and visual quality but lose camera control and the source dynamics, and that none keeps revisited places consistent. We further propose WanderLand, a training-free framework that conditions each frame of a frozen model on the same-instant source and, on revisits, on its own earlier static content. WanderLand improves five models on every dimension, by up to 8.3 points in source-dynamics fidelity.

Abstract: Vision language models (VLMs) encode substantial expert knowledge within their weights, yet their direct application to specialised classification domains such as benthic imagery remains limited. We also introduce the inverted HML score ($d_{\mathrm{inv}}$), a similarity score that evaluates hierarchical multi-label predictions through graph traversal distance and a cardinality penalty, providing an interpretable alternative to existing measures, which are insensitive to realistic degradations in sparse, deep hierarchies. Experiments on BenthicNet, FathomNet and iNaturalist show that the router improves macro $F_1$ over the vision-only baseline by up to 6.7 pp and $d_{\mathrm{inv}}$ by up to 21.1 pp on in-domain benthic data, with gains persisting under distribution shift. Abstract: Perceiving objects through fragmented occlusions, such as foliage, is a natural capability of the human visual system but remains a fundamental challenge for computer vision. This work addresses the problem of object reconstruction, or de-occlusion, in the presence of high-density, fragmented occlusions. We propose a reconstruction framework that operates on a sequence of grayscale images of a moving object, acquired by a single camera, either stationary or in motion. The algorithm estimates the object's relative positions across frames by jointly registering the images and optimizing the alignment parameters to maximize the contrast of their average, referred to as the integrated image. This integrated image serves as the reconstructed representation of the object. Experimental evaluations on both synthetic and real video sequences demonstrate the feasibility of accurate reconstruction under occlusion densities exceeding 90%. The proposed approach is competitive with synthetic aperture techniques, which are limited to static objects and require complex camera setup with careful calibration. Furthermore, we show that the integrated image enables reliable object classification under severe occlusion when used as input to standard, off-the-shelf classifiers.

Abstract: Sliced inference such as SAHI is the standard remedy for small objects in high-resolution imagery, but its gains are usually measured against low-resolution baselines. We revisit sliced inference with scale as the explicit variable and ask when tiling actually pays off. We compare seven dense detectors from four families on VisDrone and DOTA, under pre-specified hypotheses, calibration-only tuning and a single evaluation on held-out test data. Two findings emerge. First, much of the benefit attributed to slicing is a resolution effect: on VisDrone, a single pass trained at a higher resolution outperforms tiling with box fusion (merging decoded boxes) for every detector that could be trained at that resolution, and at equal compute a higher resolution helps more than a larger model. Second, tiles carry complementary information that box fusion wastes. We introduce SCAHI (Slicing with Coverage-Aware Hyper Inference), which accumulates the dense outputs of all passes with weights that give every image position the same total coverage, and reads objects off a peak grid fine enough to keep crowded neighbours apart. On identical passes, SCAHI outperforms box fusion for every YOLO detector on both datasets and matches it for FCOS. On DOTA, whose images far exceed the resolution a single pass can be trained at, tiles with SCAHI beat the best single pass for every detector, by 4.3 to 5.6 mAP50 at 2048 px, and replacing only SAHI's merge step by SCAHI adds 2.3 to 4.0 mAP50. An ablation attributes the gain to the border weighting and the resolution of the peak grid. Source code is available at https://github.com/beckerio/scahi.

At the evaluated endpoints, semantic-anchor corruption produces the largest degradation, while geometric and support responses depend on the pipeline and dataset. Our benchmark provides a reproducible protocol for diagnosing annotation robustness beyond clean-set accuracy. Abstract: Medical images encode intrinsic tissue properties that indicate disease progression, yet these properties remain entangled with the system-dependent physics of image formation. Differentiable inverse rendering asks whether they are recoverable by inverting the imaging system's forward physics rather than through post-hoc correction. We address this in optical coherence tomography (OCT), focusing on the optical attenuation coefficient, a per-pixel marker of pathology and cellular composition. Conventional recovery relies on closed-form inversions limited to simplified light-tissue models, or learned methods coupled to specific imaging configurations. We instead recover per-pixel attenuation from a single OCT image by gradient descent through a differentiable physics-based forward model. We verified the method against analytical solutions, validated it on five in vitro phantoms, and applied it without modification to 29{,}269 clinical intravascular OCT images from 128 patients. The inversion needs no training data or segmentations and is portable across platforms and retrospective archives. Replacing the forward model or recovering another optical parameter leaves the optimization procedure unchanged. We demonstrate per-pixel recovery is tractable because gradient information scales to this high-dimensional problem, where gradient-free methods become intractable. Our work supports quantitative tissue characterization from OCT images and provides a framework for extension across imaging modalities and organ systems.

Abstract: In production systems, 3D geometry is represented by primitives such as points, lines, and triangles, as rendering hardware is optimized to generate images from them very efficiently. Learning such primitives from images by volume rendering is challenging, as they are Dirac masses defined at infinitesimal locations in space. EWA splatting, Poisson reconstruction, and 3D Gaussian Splatting overcome this by convolving Dirac masses with a low-frequency kernel, a recipe that generalizes along two axes: the kernel and the primitive. As points and triangles have already received significant attention, we investigate this recipe for curvilinear primitives, and introduce Spaghetti Splatting. We approximate curves by polylines whose convolution converges to that of the continuous curve, and volume render each segment so that connected segments composite without overcounting at their joints. For fast training and inference, we linearize the camera projection per segment, which also accommodates kernels beyond Gaussians, such as Beta kernels. As a point is a curve of zero length, our representation is a strict superset of 3D Gaussian Splatting. It remains competitive with the state-of-the-art on general scenes, outperforms methods specialized to line-like geometry, natively supports anti-aliasing, and produces long connected strands that are directly amenable to physics simulation.

</details>

Abstract: Selecting an effective cover photo is a critical yet understudied problem in visual merchandising for e-commerce marketplaces. On Airbnb, hosts upload many photos depicting different rooms, amenities, and points of interest, but must manually choose a single static cover photo that is reused across heterogeneous product surfaces. This creates substantial challenges: over 90\% of listing photos have no engagement labels, leading to a severe cold-start regime; guest preferences vary across surfaces and search intents, and visually captivating photos may be semantically misaligned with the underlying listing. At Airbnb scale, this corresponds to hundreds of millions of photos and hundreds of thousands of new uploads per day.
  We propose \emph{OptiCover}, a scalable framework for cold-start visual optimization under semantic constraints and multi-surface engagement signals. The framework combines (i) a photo candidate generator that identifies candidate photos through various proxies, (ii) lightweight vision heads applied to frozen CLIP embeddings for large-scale semantic scoring, (iii) a cross-modal grounding validator based on a vision-language model (VLM) that enforces semantic representativeness, and (iv) an Exploration Logging Module (ELM) that orchestrates the collection of randomized engagement data across heterogeneous product surfaces and contextual search intents.
  We evaluate OptiCover through extensive offline studies and multiple online experiments across HomePage, and Search surfaces. OptiCover is now deployed in production and performs tens of millions of photo swaps daily. Online results show a 1.5\% engagement lift on the HomePage and a 0.35\% increase in bookers on Search, generating millions of dollars in incremental revenue. Our findings suggest that action-space shaping and semantic constraints are primary drivers of performance.
